Uniform Elgot Iteration in Foundations
Sergey Goncharov

Abstract
Category theory is famous for its innovative way of thinking of concepts by their descriptions, in particular by establishing universal properties . Concepts that can be characterized in a universal way receive a certain quality seal, which makes them easily transferable across application domains. The notion of partiality is however notoriously difficult to characterize in this way, although the importance of it is certain, especially for computer science where entire research areas, such as synthetic and axiomatic domain theory revolve around it. More recently, this issue resurfaced in the context of (constructive) intensional type theory . Here, we provide a generic categorical iteration-based notion of partiality, which is arguably the most basic one. We show that the emerging free structures, which we dub uniform-iteration algebras enjoy various desirable properties, in particular, yield an equational lifting monad . We then study the impact of classicality assumptions and choice principles on this monad, in particular, we establish a suitable categorial formulation of the axiom of countable choice entailing that the monad is an Elgot monad .

1
Introduction

Natural numbers
form a prototypical domain for programming and reasoning.
Both in
category theory
and in
type theory
they are characterized by a universal
property, which consists of two parts: a definitional principle –
(structural)
primitive recursion
and a reasoning principle –
induction
. Dualization
yields respectively
co-natural numbers
,
co-recursion
and
co-induction
.
Amid these two structuralist extremes, here, we analyse the challenging case of non-structural recursion in the form
of iteration, which arises as follows.
A map

\displaystyle h\colon S\to X+S

presents the simplest possible model of a computation process: with
S
regarded
as a state space,
h
sends any state either to a successor
state
or to
a terminal
value
in
X
. We wish to be able to form an object
KX

of
denotations
potentially reachable via such processes. Besides the
values of
X
reachable in a finite number of steps,
KX
must also contain
a designated value for divergence, generated by the right injection
h=\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}
.
We then ask: what would be the generic universal characterization of
KX
and
what properties it would imply? Somewhat surprisingly, this question has not been addressed yet on a level
of generality, sufficiently close to the settings where the question can be
posed, although many similar closely related questions have been addressed,
mostly couched in type-theoretic terms.

The question trivializes whenever one of the two following perspectives is adopted.

intensional perspective:
the domain
KX
keeps track not only
of results, but also of the number of steps needed to reach them. This
leads to the identification of
KX
as the final coalgebra
DX=\nu\gamma.\ X+\gamma
,
known as
Capretta’s monad
or the
delay monad

[
11
]
.

non-constructive perspective:
assuming non-constructive principles,
such as the
law of excluded middle
, leads to the identification of
KX
as
the
maybe-monad

X+1
.

Here, we generally keep aloof from these interpretations of
KX
and work both
extensionally and generically, using the language of the category theory to analyse
the issue in the abstract, and keeping the potential class of models possibly large.

We introduce
KX
as a certain free structure, equipped with an iteration
operator, which sends any
f\colon S\to KX+S
to
f^{\sharp}\colon S\to KX
, and satisfies
the following two basic and uncontroversial principles:

fixpoint:

f^{\sharp}
is in an obvious sense a fixpoint of
f
;

uniformity:
the structure of the state space
S
is ineffective
(i.e. merging or adding new states done coherently does not influence the result).

We dub such structures
uniform-iteration algebras

and show that on a high level of generality (in any extensive category with finite
limits and a stable natural number object) if
KX
exists then it satisfies
a number of other properties:
K
extends to a monad
{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
, which is an equational lifting
monad
[
10
]
, the Kleisli category of
K
is enriched over
partial orders and monotone maps, and the iteration operator is a least fixpoint operator w.r.t. this order;
moreover, the iteration operator satisfies an additional principle, previously
dubbed
compositionality

[
2
]
.

In some environments, such as
homotopy type theory (HoTT)
,
{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
can
be constructed directly, by using
higher inductive types
. One can then define
a universal map from the delay monad
{\bm{\mathsf{D}}}
to
{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
and regard it as a form of

extensional collapse
. However, proving
{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
to be a quotient of
{\bm{\mathsf{D}}}
seems to
be impossible without using (weak) choice principles
[
12
,
5
,
17
]
. We interpret this categorically, first by introducing a categorical
limited principle of
omniscience (LPO)
under which
{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
turns out to be isomorphic to the
maybe-monad

(\operatorname{-\!-}+1)
and also turns out to be an
Elgot monad
. This generalizes
slightly previous results
[
19
]
obtained for
hyper-extensive
categories

[
3
]
. Second, we identify other cases of
{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}

being a quotient of
{\bm{\mathsf{D}}}
and additionally being an (initial) Elgot monad, by
introducing certain coequalizer preservation conditions, abstractly capturing the
corresponding instances of the axiom of countable choice.

From the type-theoretic perspective, in our work we revisit the familiar waymarks
of using/avoiding principles of classical/constructive mathematics in view of the
tradoffs in expressive power of the corresponding constructions. Our present approach of uniform-iteration algebras as a fundamental primitive
is entirely new, though. Moreover, we would like to emphasize that our results,
being generic, apply to a wide range of categories, whose objects need not be like sets,
or types in any conventional sense. This has a massive impact on the underlying proof methods. In topos
theory, calculations are facilitated by existence of the subobject classifier
\Omega
,
which is used as a global parent space for propositions. Predicative theories, such
as HoTT make do without
\Omega
, but it is still possible to form predicative
types of propositions per universe, implying that the style of proofs
can to a significant extent be maintained, with
\Omega
intuitively regarded
as “scattered” over the cumulative universe hierarchy. Contrastingly, here we
do not assume any kind of general reference spaces for propositions, which results
in completely different proof methods. Nevertheless, we conjecture that our results
can be implemented in HoTT. This is clear for the universe of sets, which in HoTT
form a
pretopos

[
32
]
, and hence directly satisfy our
assumptions. For types of higher homotopy levels this should be possible by using
existing recipes of formalizing
precategories of types

[
35
]
.

Previous related work

We relate to the work on iteration theories, starting from a seminal paper of
Elgot
[
16
]
, who identified iteration as a fundamental unifying
notion. Equational properties of Elgot iteration were extensively explored by Bloom
and Ésik
[
9
]
with the initial iteration structure playing a prominent
role, however, since the whole setup therein is inherently classical, most of our present
agenda is essentially moot there. The uniformity property occurred under the name

functorial dagger implication
in Bloom and Ésik’s monograph, and is an established
and powerful principle, thus notably recognized in Simpson and
Plotkin’s work
[
34
]
, in the context of generic recursion (as
opposed to the present dual case of generic iteration). Adámek et al
[
2
]

introduced axioms of
(guarded) Elgot algebras
, and it follows from their results
that these axioms are complete w.r.t. the algebras of the delay monad. Uniform-iteration
algebras are generally a proper weakening of Elgot algebras, but we show that

KX
as a free uniform-iteration algebra over
X
is in fact also an Elgot algebra.

Another line of research we relate to is concerned with notions of partiality, via

dominances
, in particular the
Rosolini dominance
in
synthetic domain

theory
[
33
]
, via
equational lifting monads

[
10
]
,
and via
restriction categories

[
14
]
.
We remark that these approaches are rather concerned with specifying a notion of
partiality than with defining it. This distinction is particularly significant
in the context of constructive type theories, such as HoTT, which revitalized the interest to defining a notion of partiality
both predicatively and constructively and to understanding the impact of (restricted) choice principles.
Chapman et al
[
12
]
provided a construction of a partiality monad
as a quotient of the delay monad assuming countable choice. Also, Uustalu and
Veltri
[
37
]
explored universal properties of the obtained
quotient as an initial
\omega
-complete pointed classifying monad
.
Altenkirch et al
[
5
]
directly based on
\omega
-complete
partial orders to obtain a partiality monad in HoTT as a certain
quotient inductive-inductive type

without using any choice whatsoever, but established an equivalence with the delay
monad quotient under countable choice. Chapman et al
[
13
]
subsequently
used more basic
quotient inductive types
for the same purpose.

Recently, Escardó and Knapp
[
17
]

reinforced the issue of discrepancy between the quotient of the delay monad and
partiality monads, by showing that the quotient precisely captures extensions of
Turing computable values, whereas in the absence of any choice, the reasonable
partiality monads seem to yield properly larger carriers. The latter view
is particularly fine grained, and involves a monad, which is essentially
our monad
{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
. According to them, showing the desired connection between
{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}

and the delay monad still amounts to (very weak) choice principles (albeit still
not natively available in HoTT), while equivalence to more expressive monads would
again require countable choice. Further relevant details of type-theoretic
analysis of partiality can be found in recent theses
[
39
,
27
]
.
A comparison of various lifting monads in type theory using a unifying notion
of
container
was recently provided by Uustalu and Veltri
[
38
]
.

2
Categories and Monads

We assume familiarity with standard categorical concepts
[
30
,
6
]
.
In what follows, we generally work in an ambient extensive category
\mathbf{C}
with finite products, a stable
natural number object
{\rm Nature}
and exponentials
X^{{\rm Nature}}
. By
|\mathbf{C}|
we refer to
the objects of
\mathbf{C}
. We often drop indices of natural transformations to avoid clutter.
For the same purpose, we juxtaposition of morphisms as composition.
Let us clarify this and fix some conventions.

Extensive categories and pointful reasoning

Extensiveness means existence of disjoint finite coproducts and stability
of them under pullbacks (which must exist).
Every extensive category is
distributive
, that is, every morphism

[\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}},\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}]\colon X\times Y+X\times Z\to X\times(Y+Z)
is an isomorphism
whose inverse we denote
\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\colon X\times(Y+Z)\to X\times Y+X\times Z
. Let

\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}}\colon(X+Y)\times Z\to X\times Z+Y\times Z
be the obvious dual to
\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}
.

In order to simplify reasoning, we occasionally use a rudimentary pointful notation for stating equalities
in
\mathbf{C}
, most notably we use the case distinction operator
\operatorname{\mathsf{case}}
, e.g. we write

f(x)=\operatorname{\mathsf{case}}\mathbin{}g(x)\operatorname{\mathsf{of}}\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}}y\mapsto h(y)\operatorname{\mathsf{\kern-1.0pt;}}\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}z\mapsto u(z)

meaning
f=[h,u]\hskip 3.44444ptg
where
f\colon X\to W
,
g\colon X\to Y+Z
,
h\colon Y\to W
and

u\colon Z\to W
.

Natural numbers and primitive recursion

A
stable natural number object
(NNO) in a Cartesian category
\mathbf{C}
,
is an object
{\rm Nature}
equipped with two morphisms
\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\colon 1\to{\rm Nature}
(
zero
) and
\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}\colon{\rm Nature}\to{\rm Nature}
(
successor
)
such that for any
X,Y\in|\mathbf{C}|
and any
f\colon X\to Y
and
g\colon Y\to Y
there is unique

\operatorname{\mathsf{init}}[f,g]\colon X\times{\rm Nature}\to Y
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times{\rm Nature}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times{\rm Nature}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathsf{id},\,\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\operatorname{!}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{init}}[f,g]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{init}}[f,g]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

commutes. This combines two separate properties: there exists an initial
(1+\operatorname{-\!-})
-algebra

({\rm Nature},[\operatorname{\mathsf{o}},\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}]\colon 1+{\rm Nature}\to{\rm Nature})
, and
(X\times{\rm Nature},[\langle\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\operatorname{!}\rangle,\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}]\colon X+X\times{\rm Nature}\to X\times{\rm Nature})

is an initial
(X+\operatorname{-\!-})
-algebra. The latter property follows from the
former in Cartesian closed categories.

More generally,
we need the derivable Lawvere’s internalization of
primitive recursion

[
29
]
:
Given
f\colon X\to Y
and
g\colon Y\times X\times{\rm Nature}\to Y
there
is unique
\operatorname{\mathsf{p-rec}}(f,g)\colon X\times{\rm Nature}\to Y
such that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{p-rec}}(f,g)(x,\operatorname{\mathsf{o}})=f(x),

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{p-rec}}(f,g)(x,\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}n)=g(\operatorname{\mathsf{p-rec}}(f,g)(x,n),x,n).

We thus say that
\operatorname{\mathsf{p-rec}}(f,g)
is
defined
by
(primitive) recursion, whereas
induction
is a
proof principle
,
stating that
\operatorname{\mathsf{p-rec}}(f,g)=w
for any
w\colon X\times{\rm Nature}\to Y
satisfying the
same equations.

Exponentials
X^{\rm Nature}
are adjoint to products
X\times{\rm Nature}
, meaning that there
is an isomorphism
\operatorname{\mathsf{curry}}\colon\mathbf{C}(X\times{\rm Nature},Y)\to\mathbf{C}(X,Y^{\rm Nature})

natural in
X
. This induces an evaluation morphism
\operatorname{\mathsf{ev}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{curry}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}\mathsf{id}\colon X^{\rm Nature}\times{\rm Nature}\to X

with the standard properties.

Strong functors and monads

A functor
T
is strong if it is equipped with a natural transformation
strength

\tau_{X,Y}\colon X\times TY\to T(X\times Y)
, satisfying standard coherence conditions
w.r.t. the monoidal structure
(1,\times)
of
\mathbf{C}

[
28
]
. This induces
the obvious dual
\hat{\tau}_{X,Y}\colon TX\times Y\to T(X\times Y)
.
A natural transformation

\alpha\colon F\to G
between two strong functors is itself
strong
if it preserves
strength in the obvious sense, i.e.
\alpha\hskip 3.44444pt\tau=\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\alpha)
.

A monad
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
(in the form of a Kleisli triple) consists of an endomap
T\colon|\mathbf{C}|\to|\mathbf{C}|
,
a family of morphisms
(\eta_{X}\in\mathbf{C}(X,TX))_{X\in|\mathbf{C}|}
and a lifting operation

(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\star}\colon\mathbf{C}(X,TY)\to\mathbf{C}(TX,TY)
, satisfying standard laws
[
31
]
. It then follows
that
T
is an endofunctor with
Tf=(\eta\hskip 3.44444ptf)^{\star}
,
\eta
extends to a natural
transformation, and the
multiplication
transformation
{\mu\colon TT\to T}
is definable
as
\mathsf{id}^{\star}
.
For every monad
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
, whose underlying functor
T
is strong,
\eta
and
\mu
are
strong (with
\mathsf{id}
being a strength of
\operatorname{Id}
and
(T\tau)\hskip 3.44444pt\tau
being a strength
of
\mu
).
Such monad
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
is then called
strong
if both
\eta
and
\mu
are strong.
A strong monad is
commutative
if
\tau^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\hat{\tau}=\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau
.

We adopt Moggi’s perspective
[
31
]
to strong monads as carriers of
computational effects, and thus say that a morphism
f\colon X\to TY

computes a
value in
Y
. Since, the only effect we deal with here is divergence,
f
can
either produce a value or diverge (modulo the inherent linguistic inaccuracy of the
excluded middle law baked into the natural language).

Functor algebras and monad algebras

For an endofunctor
T
, we distinguish

T
-algebras, which are pairs
(A,a\colon TA\to A)
, from
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
-algebras, which can only be
formed for monads
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
on
T
: a
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
-algebra is a
T
-algebra
(A,a)
, which additionally
satisfies
a\hskip 3.44444pt\eta=\mathsf{id}
and
a\hskip 3.44444pt\mu=a\hskip 3.44444ptTa
. Both
T
- and
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
-algebras
form categories under the standard structure preserving morphisms, the latter
fully embeds into the former.

With our assumptions on
\mathbf{C}
, we mean to cover the following (classes of) categories.

1.

Zermello-Fraenkel set theory with choice (ZFC) and further variants of
set theory: ETCS, ZF, CZF, etc.

2.

Toposes satisfying countable choice, e.g. the
topological topos

[
25
]
.

3.

Toposes not satisfying countable choice, e.g.
nominal sets
.

4.

Pretoposes, e.g.
\Pi
W
-pretoposes, compact Hausdoff spaces.

5.

The category of topological spaces
\mathbf{Top}
, and its subcategories, such
as the category of directed complete sets
\mathbf{dCpo}
.

3
Basic Properties of the Delay Monad

The final coalgebras
DX=\nu\gamma.\,X+\gamma
jointly yield a monad
{\bm{\mathsf{D}}}
,
called the
delay monad

[
11
]
. Capretta
[
11
]

showed that
{\bm{\mathsf{D}}}
is strong,
which remains valid in our setting.
By Lambek’s lemma, the final coalgebra structure
\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\colon DX\to X+DX
is an isomorphism.
Its inverse
\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt=[\operatorname{\mathsf{now}},\operatorname{\mathsf{later}}]\colon X+DX\to DX
is composed of the morphisms, conventionally
called
\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{later}}
, of which the first one is the monad unit, and the
effect of the second one is intuitively to postpone the argument computation by
one time unit. In what follows, we will write
\lat
instead of
\operatorname{\mathsf{later}}
for the sake of
succinctness.
As a final coalgebra,
DX
comes together with a
coiteration operator
: for any

f\colon Y\to X+Y
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f\colon Y\to DX
is the unique morphism, such that

\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)=(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)\hskip 3.44444ptf
.

We denote
D1
by
\bar{\mathbb{N}}
, and think of it as an object of co-natural or possibly
infinite natural numbers. Note that the initial algebra structure

[\langle\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\operatorname{!}\rangle,\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}]\colon X+X\times{\rm Nature}\to X\times{\rm Nature}
, is an
isomorphism, and thus yields a
D
-coalgebra structure on
X\times{\rm Nature}
. This
induces a unique
D
-coalgebra morphism
\iota_{X}\colon X\times{\rm Nature}\to DX
. Alternatively,
we can regard
\iota_{X}
as defined by primitive recursion:

\displaystyle\iota_{X}(x,\operatorname{\mathsf{o}})=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}(x)

\displaystyle\iota_{X}(x,\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}(n))=

\displaystyle\;\lat(\iota_{X}(x,n))

Let
\hat{\iota}\colon{\rm Nature}\to\bar{\mathbb{N}}
be
\iota_{1}
modulo the obvious isomorphism.

In our setting,
DX
need not be postulated, for it is in fact definable as
a retract of the object
(X+1)^{\rm Nature}
of
infinite streams
, which is elaborated
in detail by Chapman et al
[
12
]
. This also entails that

\iota
is a componentwise monic. Intuitively,
DX
consists of precisely those streams, which contain at most one element of
the form
\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}}x
. This intuition becomes precise in (possibly non-classical)
set theory, where

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}x=

\displaystyle\;(\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}}x,\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}\star,\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}\star,\ldots)

\displaystyle\lat\;(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots)=

\displaystyle\;(\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}\star,e_{1},e_{2},\ldots)

This explains why classically, more precisely, under the
law of excluded
,

DX
is isomorphic to
X\times{\rm Nature}+1
. We provide a stronger result to this
effect further below. Let us record some general facts about
{\bm{\mathsf{D}}}
first.

Proposition 1
.

The monad
{\bm{\mathsf{D}}}
admits the following characterization:

1.

unit
\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\colon X\to DX
of
{\bm{\mathsf{D}}}
satisfies
\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}}
;

2.

Kleisli lifting of
f\colon X\to DY
is the unique morphism
f^{\star}\colon DX\to DY
,
for which the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X+DX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y+DY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\star}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f,~\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}f^{\star}]}

commutes;

3.

strength
\tau\colon X\times DY\to D(X\times Y)
is a unique such morphism that
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times DY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(X\times Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times(Y+DY)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y+X\times DY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y+DY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}+\tau}

commutes.

Proof.

(1) and (2) follow from a more general characterization by Uustalu
[
36
]
;
(3) is established in
[
21
]
.
∎

Proposition 2
.

{\bm{\mathsf{D}}}
is commutative.

Let us proceed with a characterization of the situations when
DX\mathbin{\cong}X\times{\rm Nature}+1
.
Recall that a monic
\sigma
is called
complemented
if there exists

\sigma^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\hookrightarrow Y
, such that
Y
is a coproduct of
X
and
X^{\prime}

with
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
as coproduct injections. The
law of excluded middle

states that any monic is complemented. We involve a rather more specific property.

Proposition 3
.

The monic
\hat{\iota}\colon{\rm Nature}\hookrightarrow\bar{\mathbb{N}}
is complemented iff
DX\mathbin{\cong}X\times{\rm Nature}+1
.

Proof (Sketch).

The necessity is obvious. Let us proceed with the sufficiency. Using extensiveness
of
\mathbf{C}
one can obtain the following pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times{\rm Nature}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Nature}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\mathbb{N}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\iota}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\operatorname{!}}

By assumption,

\hat{\iota}
is complemented, and since
\mathbf{C}
is extensive, so is
\iota
.
We obtain that
DX\mathbin{\cong}{\rm Nature}\times X+R
for some
R
, and then it follows from finality of

DX
that
R\mathbin{\cong}1
.
∎

The property of
\hat{\iota}\colon{\rm Nature}\hookrightarrow\bar{\mathbb{N}}
to be complemented is a categorical
formulation of the
limited principle of omniscience (LPO)
, which is rejected
in constructive mathematics. Informally, LPO states that every infinite bit-stream
either contains
1
at some position or contains only
0
everywhere (the constraint
that the stream contains
at most one

1
, does not make a difference). We say that
\mathbf{C}

is an LPO category if
\hat{\iota}\colon{\rm Nature}\hookrightarrow\bar{\mathbb{N}}
is complemented.

Corollary 4
.

Suppose that (i)
\mathbf{C}
has countable products and (ii) given a family
({\sigma_{i}\colon A_{i}\to A})_{i\in\omega}

of complemented pairwise disjoint monos, the induced universal morphism

\coprod_{i}A_{i}\to A
is complemented. Then
\mathbf{C}
satisfies LPO and hence
DX\mathbin{\cong}X\times{\rm Nature}+1
.

Proof.

It is folklore that in categories with countable products
{\rm Nature}

is isomorphic to the sum of
\omega
copies of
1
. Thus
\hat{\iota}\colon{\rm Nature}\to\bar{\mathbb{N}}

is the induced universal map, which is complemented by (ii).
∎

Example 5
.

As expected,
\cref
prop:D-class does not apply to models, designed with constructivist
principles in mind, such as intensional type theories, or realizability toposes,
although, it is technically possible to design a realizability topos,
satisfying LPO
[
8
]
, in which thus
DX\mathbin{\cong}X\times{\rm Nature}+1
. Another class of examples to which
\cref
prop:D-class
does not apply stems from topology. In
\mathbf{Top}
,
\bar{\mathbb{N}}
is a subspace of the
Cantor
space

2^{\rm Nature}
whose topology is generated by the base of opens of the form

\{sr\mid r\in\{0,1\}^{\omega}\}
with
s\in 2^{\star}
. Then
\bar{\mathbb{N}}
is isomorphic to
a
one-point compactification
of
{\rm Nature}
, i.e. it is the set
{\rm Nature}\cup\{\infty\}
,
whose opens are all subsets of
{\rm Nature}
and additionally all complements of finite
subsets of
{\rm Nature}
in
{\rm Nature}\cup\{\infty\}
. Clearly,
\bar{\mathbb{N}}\ncong{\rm Nature}+1
. This kind of arguments
is inherited by higher order topology-based models, such as Johnstone’s
topological
topos

[
25
]
, which is a Grothendieck topos not satisfying LPO.

Example 6
.

\cref

prop:D-class and
\cref
cor:D-class cover quite a few models constructed
in the scope of classical mathematics. Every set theory satisfying the law of
excluded middle satisfies LPO. Every presheaf topos (w.r.t. a classical set theory)
inherits countable coproducts from
\mathbf{Set}
and those satisfy (ii) of
\cref
cor:D-class.
As we indicated in
\cref
exa:D-neg, a Grothendieck topos generally need not
satisfy LPO, but, e.g.
Schanuel topos
(aka the topos of nominal sets)
does satisfy it, because this topos is Boolean.
As we indicated in
\cref
exa:D-neg,
\mathbf{Top}
does not satisfy LPO, but curiously
the full subcategory of directed complete partial orders
\mathbf{dCpo}
(under Scott topology)
does. Both
\mathbf{Top}
and
\mathbf{dCpo}
have countable coproducts, but
\mathbf{Top}
fails to
satisfy condition (ii), of
\cref
cor:D-class, while
\mathbf{dCpo}
does satisfy it.
This can be read as a manifestation of (undesirable) effects, which motivated synthetic
domain theory
[
23
]
.

Conditions (i) and (ii) in
\cref
cor:D-class are essentially the axioms of

hyper-extensive categories
by Adámek et al
[
3
]

(modulo our background extensiveness assumption). An example of an LPO category
that fails (i) is Lawvere’s ETCS. Another example of a Grothendieck topos that
fails (ii) can be rendered as a certain category of
Jónsson-Tarski
algebras

[
3
]
.

The above examples indicate that in models developed w.r.t. constructive foundations
LPO fails by design, while in models developed w.r.t. classical foundations,
depending on the purposes, constructively questioned principles may leak in from the
metalogic level inside of the category, possibly in a weakened form, resulting
in an explicit expression for
DX
.

4
Unguarded Elgot Algebras

Recall the following notion from
[
2
]
where the term

complete Elgot algebra over
H
is used.

Definition 7

(Guarded Elgot Algebras)
.

Given an endofunctor
H
, an
(
H
-)guarded Elgot algebra
is a tuple
(A,a\colon HA\to A,(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\sharp})

where the
iteration

f^{\sharp}\colon X\to A
for every given
f\colon X\to A+HX
,
satisfies the following axioms:

(
Fixpoint
)
for every
f\colon X\to A+HX
,
f^{\sharp}=[\mathsf{id},a\hskip 3.44444ptHf^{\sharp}]\hskip 3.44444ptf
;

(
Uniformity
)
for every
f\colon X\to A+HX
every
g\colon Y\to A+HY
and every
h\colon X\to Y
,

(\mathsf{id}+Hh)\hskip 3.44444ptf=g\hskip 3.44444pth
implies
f^{\sharp}=g^{\sharp}\hskip 3.44444pth
;

(
Compositionality
)
for every
h\colon Y\to X+HY
and
f\colon X\to A+HX
,
((f^{\sharp}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pth)^{\sharp}=\bigl([(\mathsf{id}+H\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}})\hskip 3.44444ptf,\operatorname{}\linebreak\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(H\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}})]\hskip 3.44444pt{[\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}},\operatorname{}\linebreak h]}\colon X+Y\to A+H(X+Y)\bigr)^{\sharp}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}
.

H
-guarded Elgot algebras form a category together with
iteration preserving

morphisms defined as follows: a morphism
h
from
(A,a,(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\sharp})
to

(B,b,(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\sharp})
is a morphism
h\colon A\to B
between carriers, such
that
h\hskip 3.44444ptf^{\sharp}=((h+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444ptf)^{\sharp}

for every
f\colon X\to A+HX

(this entails
h\hskip 3.44444pta=b\hskip 3.44444pt(Hh)

[
2
, Lemma 5.2]
).

The
Compositionality
axiom is the most sophisticated one. It intuitively states that
running
h
in a loop over
Y
as the state space, and subsequently running

f
in a loop over
X
as the state space, equivalently corresponds to running
a certain term constructed from
f
and
g
in a single loop over the combined state
X+Y
.

The axioms of guarded Elgot algebras are complete in the following sense.

Theorem 8
.

[
2
, Theorem 5.4,Corollary 5.7,Theorem 5.8]

For every
X
, a final coalgebra
\nu\gamma.\,X+H\gamma
is a free
H
-guarded algebra
over
X
, in particular, existence of final coalgebras is equivalent to existence
of free
H
-guarded Elgot algebras.
The categories of
H
-guarded Elgot algebras and
algebras of the monad
\nu\gamma.\,X+H\gamma
are isomorphic.

By Theorem
8
, free algebras of the delay monad are thus precisely the free
\operatorname{Id}
-guarded
Elgot algebras. We then introduce
un-guarded Elgot algebras
as a certain subcategory
of
\operatorname{Id}
-guarded ones.

Definition 9

(Unguarded Elgot Algebras)
.

We call
\operatorname{Id}
-guarded Elgot algebras of the form
(A,\mathsf{id}\colon A\to A,(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\sharp})

unguarded Elgot algebras
, or simply
Elgot algebras
if no confusion arises.
Given two Elgot algebras
A
and
B
, we call
f\colon X\times A\to B
right iteration
preserving if

\displaystyle f(\mathsf{id}\times h^{\sharp})=\bigl(X\times Z\,\xrightarrow{\mathsf{id}\times h}\,X\times(A+Z)\,\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}}\,X\times A+X\times Z\,\xrightarrow{f+\mathsf{id}}\,B+X\times Z\bigr)^{\sharp}

for any
h\colon Z\to A+Z
. This generalizes Elgot algebra morphisms under
X=1
.

We write simply ‘iteration preserving’ instead of ‘right iteration preserving’
in the sequel if the decomposition of
X\times A
into the Elgot algebra part
A

and the parameter part
X
is clear from the context. Parametrization will be
needed later for characterizing stability of free algebras (Lemma
18
).

The unguarded Elgot algebras thus differ from the
\operatorname{Id}
-guarded ones
in that the
\operatorname{Id}
-algebra structures
a\colon A\to A
in the former case are forced to be
trivial. This has an impact on forming the corresponding free structures: in the
guarded case, the
\operatorname{Id}
-algebra structures must be maximally unrestricted, which is the
reason why we obtain a free
\operatorname{Id}
-guarded Elgot algebra
DX
with the
\operatorname{Id}
-algebra
structure playing the role of delays. Intuitively, a free unguarded Elgot algebra must be a quotient of a free
guarded one under removing delays, which is indeed what happens for LPO categories,
as we show later. Otherwise, the situation is much more subtle, and it is one of
our goals to demonstrate that free unguarded Elgot algebras are exactly the
semantic carriers generated by unguarded iteration.

In the unguarded case
Compositionality
can be replaced by a simpler looking new law that we dub
Folding
:

Proposition 10
.

Given
A\in|\mathbf{C}|
,
(A,(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\sharp})
is an Elgot algebra
iff
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\sharp}
satisfies

(
Fixpoint
)
for every
f\colon X\to A+X
,
f^{\sharp}=[\mathsf{id},f^{\sharp}]\hskip 3.44444ptf
;

(
Uniformity
)
for every
f\colon X\to A+X
every
g\colon Y\to A+Y
and every
h\colon X\to Y
,

(\mathsf{id}+h)\hskip 3.44444ptf=g\hskip 3.44444pth
implies
f^{\sharp}=g^{\sharp}\hskip 3.44444pth
;

(
Folding
)
for every
h\colon Y\to X+Y
and
f\colon X\to A+X
,

(f^{\sharp}+h)^{\sharp}=[(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}})\hskip 3.44444ptf,\operatorname{}\linebreak\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}\hskip 3.44444pth]^{\sharp}
.

The laws of Elgot algebras are summarised in Fig.
1
in the style
of string diagrams, akin to those, which are used for axiomatizing traced symmetric
monoidal categories
[
26
]
. In contrast to the latter, here we
essentially can only form traces of morphisms of the form
X+Y\to A+Y
where
A

is an Elgot algebra. Merging wires is to be interpreted as calling codiagonal
morphisms
\nabla\colon X+X\to X
.

(d)
Folding

Figure 1:
Laws of (unguarded) Elgot algebras.

As expected, products and exponents of Elgot algebras can be formed in a canonical way.

Lemma 11
.

Given two Elgot algebras
(A,(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\sharp})
and
(B,(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\sharp})

and an object
X\in|\mathbf{C}|
,

1.

(A\times B,(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\hat{\sharp}})
is an Elgot algebra with

h^{\hat{\sharp}}=\langle((\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pth)^{\sharp},((\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pth)^{\sharp}\rangle
for any
h\colon Z\to A\times B+Z
.

2.

If
A^{X}
exists then
(A^{X},(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\hat{\sharp}})
is an
Elgot algebra with

h^{\hat{\sharp}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{curry}}((\operatorname{\mathsf{ev}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}}\hskip 3.44444pt(h\times\mathsf{id}))^{\sharp}
for any
h\colon Z\to A^{X}+Z
.

Every Elgot algebra
(A,(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\sharp})
comes together with a divergence
constant
\bot\colon 1\to A=(\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}\colon 1\to A+1)^{\sharp}
. Note that
\bot
is automatically
preserved by Elgot algebra morphisms.

By omitting the not entirely self-motivating
Compositionality
(or
Folding
) law, we obtain what we dub

uniform-iteration algebras
. As we see later, this law is automatic
for free uniform-iteration algebras.

Definition 12

(Uniform-Iteration Algebras)
.

A
uniform-iteration algebra
is a tuple
(A,(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\sharp})
as in

\cref
def:uea but
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\sharp}
is only required to satisfy

Fixpoint
and
Uniformity
. Morphisms of uniform-iteration algebras are defined in the same way.

5
The Initial Pre-Elgot Monad

The goal of this section is to show that free uniform-iteration algebras
coincide with free Elgot algebras (Theorem
29
), and enjoy a number of other
characteristic properties. In particular, we characterize the functor sending
any
X
to a free uniform-iteration algebra on
X
as an initial
pre-Elgot monad. We define pre-Elgot monads as follows.

Definition 13

(Pre-Elgot Monads)
.

We call a monad
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}

pre-Elgot
if every
TX
is equipped with an Elgot
algebra structure, in such a way that
h^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptf^{\sharp}=((h^{\star}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444ptf)^{\sharp}

for any
f\colon Z\to TX+Z
and any
h\colon X\to TY
.
A pre-Elgot monad
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
is
strong pre-Elgot
if
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
is strong as a monad
and strength is iteration preserving.

Pre-Elgot monads are to be compared with Elgot monads, which support a stronger
type profile for the iteration operator, and satisfy more sophisticated axioms.

Definition 14

(Elgot Monads
[
16
,
4
]
)
.

A monad
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
is an
Elgot monad
if it is equipped with an iteration
operator sending each
f\colon X\to T(Y+X)
to
f^{\dagger}\colon X\to TY
and
satisfying:

(
Fixpoint
)

f^{\dagger}=[\eta,f^{\dagger}]^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptf
;

(
Naturality
)

g^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptf^{\dagger}=([(T\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}})\hskip 3.44444ptg,\operatorname{}\linebreak\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}]^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptf)^{\dagger}
for
f\colon X\to T(Y+X)
,
g\colon Y\to TZ
;

(
Codiagonal
)

(T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}]\hskip 3.44444ptf)^{\dagger}=f^{\dagger\dagger}
for
f\colon X\to T((Y+X)+X)
;

(
Uniformity
)

f\hskip 3.44444pth=T(\mathsf{id}+h)\hskip 3.44444ptg
implies

f^{\dagger}\hskip 3.44444pth=g^{\dagger}
for
f\colon X\to T(Y+X)
,
g\colon Z\to T(Y+Z)
and

h\colon Z\to X
.

If
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
is additionally strong then
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
is strong Elgot if moreover:

(
Strength
)

\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times f^{\dagger})=((T\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times f))^{\dagger}
for any
f\colon X\to T(Y+X)
.

Proposition 15
.

(Strong) Elgot monads are (strong) pre-Elgot under
f^{\sharp}=([T\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}},\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}]\hskip 3.44444ptf)^{\dagger}
.

It has been argued
[
19
,
22
]

that strong Elgot monads are minimal semantic structures for interpreting effectful
while-languages. In that sense, we acknowledge an expressivity
gap between Elgot and pre-Elgot monads, which generally happen to be too weak.
We will consider approaches to close this gap, in particular by drawing on some
versions of the axiom of countable choice. Even though, in general, the gap presumably
cannot be closed, we regard the initial pre-Elgot monad to be an important notion,
which arises from first principles and carries a very clear operational intuition.
The discrepancy between pre-Elgot monads and Elgot monads seems to represent a
very basic form of discrepancy between operational and denotational semantics.
We thus find it important to conceptually delineate between Elgot monads and
pre-Elgot monads, no matter how desirable it is to have them to be equivalent.

Lemma 16
.

If for every
X\in|\mathbf{C}|
a free uniform-iteration algebra
KX
exists then

K
extends to a monad
{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
whose algebras are precisely uniform-iteration algebras.

As in the case of natural numbers, one cannot make much progress without stability.

Definition 17

(Stable Free Uniform-Iteration Algebras)
.

A free uniform-iteration algebra
KY
over
Y
is
stable
if
for every
X\in|\mathbf{C}|
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}\colon X\times KY\to X
is a free uniform-iteration
algebra in the slice category
\mathbf{C}/X
.

Lemma 18
.

For
Y\in|\mathbf{C}|
,
KY
is stable iff for every uniform-iteration

A
and every
f\colon X\times Y\to A
, there is unique iteration preserving

f^{\hash}\colon X\times KY\to A
such that
{f=f^{\hash}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\eta)}
.

Using
\cref
lem:prod_exp, it is easy to show that in Cartesian closed categories
every
KX
is stable.
For the rest of the section, we assume that all free
uniform-iteration algebras
KX
exist and are stable.

Proposition 19
.

The monad
{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
is
strong, with the components
of strength
\tau\colon X\times KY\to K(X\times Y)
uniquely identified
by the conditions:

\displaystyle\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\eta)=\;\eta,

\displaystyle\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times h^{\sharp})=\;((\tau+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times h))^{\sharp}

\displaystyle(h\colon Z\to KY+Z)

Proof.

In the notation of
\cref
lem:stable we define strength of
{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
as

(\eta\colon X\times Y\to K(X\times Y))^{\hash}
. The axioms of strength are easy to verify.
∎

As a next step, we show that
{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
is an equational lifting monad in the sense
of Bucalo et al
[
10
]
. This means precisely that
{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
is
commutative and satisfies the equational law:

\displaystyle\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\Delta=K\langle\eta,\mathsf{id}\rangle.

(1)

This law is rather restrictive, and roughly means that some form
of non-termination is the only possible effect of the monad. Proving (
1
)
is nontrivial. The key step is the following property, which allows
for splitting a loop involving a product of algebras into two loops.

Lemma 20
.

Given uniform-iteration algebras
A,B
and
C
,
f\colon Z\to{A\times B}+Z
and
{h\colon A\times B\to C}
,

((h+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444ptf)^{\sharp}=((h+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times(\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444ptf))^{\sharp}\hskip 3.44444pt\langle((\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444ptf)^{\sharp},\mathsf{id}\rangle
.

Lemma 21
.

Given
X,Z\in|\mathbf{C}|
, and
h\colon Z\to KX+Z
, then

\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle h^{\sharp},h^{\sharp}\rangle=((\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\Delta+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pth)^{\sharp}
.

Proof.

It follows from
\cref
lem:key that

((\tau+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times h))^{\sharp}\hskip 3.44444pt\langle h^{\sharp},\mathsf{id}\rangle=((\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\Delta+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pth)^{\sharp}
.
On the other hand, by
\cref
prop:tau-prop,

((\tau+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times h))^{\sharp}\hskip 3.44444pt\langle h^{\sharp},\mathsf{id}\rangle=\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle h^{\sharp},h^{\sharp}\rangle
.
By combining the last two identities, we obtain the goal.
∎

Theorem 22
.

{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
is an equational lifting monad.

Proof.

Let us sketch the proof of (
1
).
Since
K\langle\eta,\mathsf{id}\rangle=(\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\eta,\mathsf{id}\rangle)^{\star}
, using the definition
of Kleisli star for
K
, it suffices to show that
\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\Delta
is a unique
iteration preserving morphism for which
\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\eta,\mathsf{id}\rangle=\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\Delta\hskip 3.44444pt\eta
.
Indeed,
\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\Delta\hskip 3.44444pt\eta=\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\eta)\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\eta,\mathsf{id}\rangle=\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\eta,\mathsf{id}\rangle
, and

\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\Delta
is iteration preserving by
\cref
lem:tau-Delta.
∎

The fact that
{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
is an equational lifting monad has a number of implications,
in particular, the Kleisli category of
{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
is a
restriction category

[
14
]
.
That is, we can calculate the
domain (of definiteness)
, represented by an idempotent
Kleisli morphism as follows: given
f\colon X\to KY
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}f=(K\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},f\rangle\colon X\to KX,

We additionally use the notation
f\downharpoonright g=\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle f,g\rangle
, meaning:
restrict
f
to the domain of
g
. It is easy to see that
\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}f=\eta\downharpoonright f

and
f\downharpoonright g=f^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}g)
. Let
f\sqsubseteq g
abbreviate

f=g\downharpoonright f
. Under this definition, every
\mathbf{C}(X,KY)
is partially ordered,
which is a general fact about restriction categories. In our case, moreover, this
partial order additionally has a bottom element
\bot=\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}^{\sharp}
;

\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}(\eta\hskip 3.44444ptf)=\eta
for any
f\colon X\to KY
, and
\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}f\sqsubseteq\eta
for any
f
.

Proposition 23
.

The Kleisli category of
{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
is enriched over pointed partial orders
and strict monotone maps. Moreover, strength preserves
\bot
and
\sqsubseteq
as follows:

\displaystyle\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\bot)=\bot

\displaystyle f\sqsubseteq g\text{\hskip 20.44434ptimplies\hskip 20.44434pt}\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times f)\sqsubseteq\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times g)

Corollary 24
.

K\iobj\mathbin{\cong}1
.

Proof.

Since
\operatorname{!}\bot=\mathsf{id}\colon 1\to 1
and
\bot\operatorname{!}=\mathsf{id}\colon K\iobj\to K\iobj
,
we obtain an isomorphism
K\iobj\mathbin{\cong}1
.
∎

Proposition 25
.

The monad
{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
is
copyable
and
weakly discardable

[
20
]
,
i.e.:

\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\Delta=K\Delta
and
(K\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}})\hskip 3.44444pt\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle f,g\rangle\sqsubseteq f

for
f\colon X\to KY
and
g\colon X\to KZ
.

Definition 26

(Bounded Iteration)
.

Let
A
be a
pointed object
, i.e. an object with a canonical map

\bot\colon 1\to A
. Then we define
bounded iteration

(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\langle\kern-0.8pt\sharp}\colon\mathbf{C}(X,A+X)\to\mathbf{C}(X\times{\rm Nature},A)
by primitive recursion as follows:

\displaystyle f^{{\langle\kern-0.8pt\sharp}}(x,\operatorname{\mathsf{o}})=\bot

\displaystyle f^{{\langle\kern-0.8pt\sharp}}(x,\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}n)=\operatorname{\mathsf{case}}\mathbin{}f(x)\operatorname{\mathsf{of}}\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}}a\mapsto a\operatorname{\mathsf{\kern-1.0pt;}}\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}y\mapsto f^{{\langle\kern-0.8pt\sharp}}(y,n).

Intuitively,
f^{{\langle\kern-0.8pt\sharp}}(x,n)
behaves as

f^{\sharp}(x)
except that at each iteration the counter
n
is decreased, and

\bot
is returned once
n=\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}
. We next show that
f^{\sharp}(x)
is in a suitable sense a limit of the
f^{{\langle\kern-0.8pt\sharp}}(x,n)

as
n
tends to infinity. This is, of course, a form of
Kleene fixpoint theorem
.

Theorem 27

(Kleene Fixpoint Theorem)
.

Given
f\colon X\to KY+X
, and
g\colon X\to KY
,

(i)
f^{{\langle\kern-0.8pt\sharp}}\sqsubseteq f^{\sharp}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}
, and
(ii)
f^{{\langle\kern-0.8pt\sharp}}\sqsubseteq g\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}
implies
f^{\sharp}\sqsubseteq g
.

Corollary 28
.

Given
f\colon X\to KY+X
,
f^{\sharp}\colon X\to KY
is the least pre-fixpoint of the
map
[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{-\!-}]\hskip 3.44444ptf\colon\mathbf{C}(X,KY)\to\mathbf{C}(X,KY)
.

Finally, we obtain

Theorem 29
.

{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
is an initial pre-Elgot monad and an initial strong pre-Elgot monad.

6
Quotienting the Delay Monad

By
\cref
thm:D,
\operatorname{Id}
-guarded Elgot algebras are precisely the
{\bm{\mathsf{D}}}
-algebras. We
proceed to characterize uniform-iteration and Elgot algebras as certain
D
-algebras,
which we dub
search-algebras
. Intuitively, modulo identification of
DA

with a set of streams from
(A+1)^{\rm Nature}
, a search-algebra structure
a\colon DA\to A

is guaranteed to find the first element in the stream of the form
\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}}a
if it
exists. We expect that this notion can formulated more generally, but we do not pursue it here.

Definition 30

(Search-Algebra)
.

We call a
D
-algebra
(A,a\colon DA\to A)
a
search-algebra
if it satisfies
the conditions:
a\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}=\mathsf{id}
,

a\hskip 3.44444pt\lat=a
.
Search-algebras form a full subcategory of the category of all
D
-algebras.

Uniform-iteration algebras capture the structure of search-algebras
independently of the assumption that
D
exists. This and further connections
between categories of
D
-algebras illustrated in Fig.
2
(arrows indicate full embeddings of categories)
are formalized as follows.

D
-algebras

uniform-iteration algebras/

search-algebras

{\bm{\mathsf{D}}}
-algebras

Elgot algebras

Figure 2
:
Connections between classes of
D
-algebras.

Proposition 31
.

1.

The categories of uniform-iteration algebras and search-algebras are isomorphic
under:

\displaystyle(A,(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\sharp})

\displaystyle\;\mapsto(A,\operatorname{}\linebreak\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\sharp}\colon DA\to A),

\displaystyle(A,a\colon DA\to A)

\displaystyle\;\mapsto(A,\operatorname{}\linebreak a\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{-\!-})\colon\mathbf{C}(X,A+X)\to\mathbf{C}(X,A)).

2.

Elgot algebras are precisely those
D
-algebras, which are
search-algebras and
{\bm{\mathsf{D}}}
-algebras.

Lemma 32
.

Every Elgot algebra
(DA,a\colon DA\to A)
satisfies
a\hskip 3.44444pt\iota^{\star}=a\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}})
.

We proceed to model the construction of quotienting
{\bm{\mathsf{D}}}

by weak bisimilarity
\approx
, previously described in type-theoretic
terms
[
12
]
. Modulo identification of
DX
with the object
of those streams
\sigma\colon{\rm Nature}\to X+1
for which
\sigma(n)\neq\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}\star
for at most
one
n
,
\approx
can be described as follows:
\sigma\approx\sigma^{\prime}

if for every
a
,
\sigma(n)=a
for some
n
iff
\sigma^{\prime}(n)=a
for some
n
.

Recall the embedding
\iota\colon X\times{\rm Nature}\hookrightarrow DX
, and define the
quotient of
DX
by the coequalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(X\times{\rm Nature})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\star}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{X}}

(2)

which we assume to exist and be preserved by products.
It is then straightforward that
{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}

is a functor and
\rho_{X}
is natural in
X
. It also follows that
X\,\xrightarrow{~\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}~}\,DX\,\xrightarrow{~\rho~}\,{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X
is strong.
Following tradition, we denote
{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}1
as
\Sigma
.

Lemma 33
.

\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\lat=\rho
.

Defining
\rho
as a coequalizer of
\lat
and
\mathsf{id}
in the first place does not
seem to be sufficient, though, in particular, for showing the following property.
We leave open the question of identifying conditions under which it is
possible.

Proposition 34
.

The following is a coequalizer:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(X+(X\times{\rm Nature}+X\times{\rm Nature}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\eta,\,[\eta\hskip 2.41112pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},\,\iota\hskip 2.41112pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{s}})]]}^{\star}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\eta,\,[\iota\hskip 2.41112pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}),\,\eta\hskip 2.41112pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}]]}^{\star}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{X}}

(3)

The last proposition brings the definition of
\rho
in accordance with the
intuition; the coproduct
X+(X\times{\rm Nature}+X\times{\rm Nature})
covers three alternatives
for
\sigma\approx\sigma^{\prime}
: either
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
, or
\sigma
terminates earlier
than
\sigma^{\prime}
by a specified number, or the other way around. It can be verified
that the embedding
D(X+(X\times{\rm Nature}+X\times{\rm Nature}))\hookrightarrow DX\times DX
is an
internal equivalence relation.

Theorem 35
.

The following conditions are equivalent:

1.

for every
X
, coequalizer (
2
) is preserved by
D
;

2.

every
{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X
extends to a search-algebra,
so that each
\rho_{X}
is a
D
-algebra morphism;

3.

for every
X
,
({\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X,\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\colon X\to{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X)
is
a stable free Elgot algebra on
X
,
\rho_{X}
is a
D
-algebra morphism and

\rho_{X}=((\rho_{X}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\sharp}
;

4.

{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}
extends to a strong monad, so that
\rho
is a strong monad morphism.

If the equivalent conditions of Theorem
35
are satisfied, we obtain
an explicit construction of the initial pre-Elgot monad
{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
, which we explored
previously. Let us consider concrete examples.

Example 36

(Maybe-Monad)
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{C}
is an LPO category, and recall that
DX
is isomorphic to

X\times{\rm Nature}+1
. It is then easy to check that (
2
)
exists, it is preserved by products,
{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X\mathbin{\cong}X+1
and
\rho=\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\mathsf{id}\colon X+1\to X\times{\rm Nature}+1
. Since

D
is the composition of
(\operatorname{-\!-}\times{\rm Nature})
and
(\operatorname{-\!-}+1)
, and both these
functors preserve coequalizers (first as a left adjoint, and second by extensiveness
of
\mathbf{C}
),
D
preserves (
2
). We thus obtain that the maybe-monad
is an initial pre-Elgot monad. This covers instances of LPO categories from
\cref
exa:D-pos.
Moreover, the initial pre-Elgot monad is in fact an initial Elgot monad in this
case: the profiles of the iteration operators
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\sharp}
and
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\dagger}

agree up to rearrangement of summands, and the axioms of

\cref
def:elgot-monad become the axioms of
\cref
def:pem, except for
Codiagonal
,
which can be checked directly.

Note that Example
36
entails that the maybe-monad is the initial
Elgot monad in
\mathbf{dCpo}
. This is a result of our assumption that
\mathbf{dCpo}
is developed
w.r.t. a classical set theory, which entails that
\mathbf{dCpo}
is an LPO category.
This would not be the case if we defined
\mathbf{dCpo}
internally to a non-classical
environment, which is indeed the core idea of synthetic domain theory.

Another direction for obtaining an Elgot monad
from (
2
) is by
using a suitable instance of the
axiom of countable choice
. In our setting
this takes the following form.

Theorem 37
.

Suppose that the coequalizers (
2
) are preserved by the
exponentiation
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\rm Nature}
.

1.

The equivalent conditions of
\cref
thm:Drho hold, in particular,

{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{{\bm{\mathsf{D}}}}}
is an initial (strong) pre-Elgot monad.

2.

If every (
3
) is an effective quotient,
i.e.
D(X+(X\times{\rm Nature}+X\times{\rm Nature}))
is a kernel pair of
\rho_{X}
, then
{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{{\bm{\mathsf{D}}}}}

is a strong Elgot monad with
f^{\dagger}
being the least fixpoint of

[\eta,\operatorname{-\!-}]^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptf\colon\mathbf{C}(X,{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}Y)\to\mathbf{C}(X,{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}Y)
for any
f\colon X\to{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}(Y+X)
.

The effectiveness assumption in clause
2
.
is satisfied in any exact
category (e.g. in any pretopos) – by definition, every internal equivalence
relation there is effective.

Example 38
.

Theorem
37
applies to
\mathbf{Top}
, yielding a concrete description for
{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
.
Recall that in
\mathbf{Top}
, coequalizers are computed as in
\mathbf{Set}
and are equipped
with the quotient topology. Note that
DX
is the set
X\times{\rm Nature}\cup\{\infty\}

whose base opens are
\{(x,n)\mid x\in O\}
and
\{(x,k)\mid x\in X,k\geq n\}\cup\{\infty\}

with
n\in{\rm Nature}
and
O
ranging over the opens of
X
. The collapse
{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X

computed with (
2
) is thus the set
X\cup\{\infty\}
, whose opens
are those of
X
and additionally the entire space
X\cup\{\infty\}
, in particular,

{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}1
is the
Sierpiński space
.

To obtain that (
2
)
is preserved by
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\rm Nature}
, it suffices to show that the opens of

(X\cup\{\infty\})^{\rm Nature}
are precisely those, whose inverse images under

\rho^{\rm Nature}
are open. This is in fact true for any regular epi in
\mathbf{Top}
. The
effectiveness condition in
2
.
is not vacuous for

\mathbf{Top}
, which is not an exact category (and not even regular), but it can be checked
manually.

In every pretopos, preservation of (
2
) by
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\rm Nature}

is a proper instance of the
internal axiom of countable choice
, or
internal
projectivity of
{\rm Nature}
, which means preservation of epis by

(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\rm Nature}
, roughly because every pretopos is
exact
and our quotienting
morphism
\rho
is associated with an internal equivalence relation by
\cref
lem:bis.

\cref
thm:elg can thus be related to the existing result in
synthetic domain theory, that Rosolini dominance, i.e. our
\Sigma
, is indeed
a dominance
[
33
]
, which applies to Hyland’s
effective topos

[
24
]
,
as it satisfies countable choice. Contrastingly, we cannot apply
\cref
thm:elg to nominal
sets, which falsify countable choice, however, as a
Boolean topos, nominal sets fall into the scope of
\cref
exa:maybe.

We currently do not have a concrete example of
{\bm{\mathsf{K}}}
being definable, but not
being an Elgot monad. Theorem
37
and Example
36
suggests
that a non-artificial category to witness this must neither support excluded middle nor the axiom
of countable choice.

7
Conclusions and Further Work

Iteration and iteration theories emerged as unifying concepts for computer
science semantics and reasoning. By interpreting iteration suitably,
one obtains a basic extensible equational logic of programs, shown to be sound and complete
across various models
[
9
]
. Elgot monads implement
this inherently algebraic view in the general categorical realm
of abstract data types and effects. The class of Elgot monads (over a fixed category)
is stable under various categorical constructions (monad transformers), and thus one can build
new Elgot monads from old, but the most simple Elgot monad, the initial one, does
not arise in this way.

Here, we proposed an approach to defining
an initial iteration structure from first principles, characterized it in
various ways, analysed conditions, under which it can be concretely described,
and to yield an Elgot monad. Unsurprisingly, these conditions
generally cannot be lifted, as the previous research in type theory indicates.
We consider broadening the scope in which results about notions of partiality apply,
and unifying both classical and non-classical models, as an important part of our
contribution. Universal properties play a central role in category theory,
but many important concepts are not covered by them. One example is
Sierpiński space, which is fundamental in topology, duality theory
and domain theory. It follows from our results, that it is in fact a free
uniform-iteration algebra on one generator. We believe that the structure of
our results can be reused in more sophisticated setting, such as semantics
of
hybrid systems
, which require a notion of partiality, combined with continuous
evolution, and rise semantic issues, structurally similar to those, we considered
here
[
15
]
. Another potential for taking further
the present work is to consider more general shapes of the basic functor (instead of
the current
(X+\operatorname{-\!-})
), prospectively leading to more sophisticated (non-)structural
recursion scenarios (see e.g.
[
1
]
).
