\displaystyle\;=[\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}},\,\lat\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}](\mathsf{id}+\lat u)\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle\;=[\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}},\,\lat\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\hskip 3.44444pt\lat u]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle\;=[\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}},\,\lat\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}u]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle\;=D(\mathsf{id}+u)\hskip 3.44444pt[\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}},\,\lat\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}.

By uniformity,
w^{\sharp}u=([\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}},\,\lat\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}]\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\sharp}
. But
\mathsf{id}

satisfies the fixpoint equation for
w^{\sharp}u
:

\displaystyle[\operatorname{\mathsf{now}},\mathsf{id}]^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt[\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}},\,\lat\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}]\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}=[\operatorname{\mathsf{now}},\lat[\operatorname{\mathsf{now}},\mathsf{id}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}]\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}=[\operatorname{\mathsf{now}},\lat]\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}=\mathsf{id}.

Therefore
w^{\sharp}u=\mathsf{id}
.
∎

Lemma 48
.

Let
(A,a)
be a search-algebra. Then for any
X
,
\tau_{A,X}\colon A\times DX\to D(A\times X)

is a retraction, whose section is
\langle a\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}),D\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}\rangle\colon D(A\times X)\to A\times DX
.

Proof.

Using
\cref
lem:D-proj, we proceed to show that

\displaystyle\langle D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},D\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}\rangle=\langle D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},D\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}\rangle\hskip 3.44444pt\tau_{A,X}\hskip 3.44444pt\langle a\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}),D\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}\rangle,

which is equivalent to
D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}=(Da)\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},D\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}\rangle

and
D\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}=D\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}
. To obtain the former equation, we show commutativity of the
diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(A\times X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times X+D(A\times X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DA\times DX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DA\times(X+DX)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DA\times X+DA\times DX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(DA\times X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DA\times X+D(DA\times X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DDA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DA+DDA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A+DA}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},D\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\times\mathsf{id}+\langle\lat\hskip 2.41112pt(D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}),D\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}+\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Da}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a+Da}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}}

Except for the top cell, the remaining ones commute by definition. Assume
commutativity of the top cell for the time being. Then the composition on the
right vertical edge is easily seen to be equal to

\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+(Da)\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},D\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}\rangle
. The resulting diagram
then witnesses the fact that
(Da)\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},D\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}\rangle

is a coalgebra morphism, which must be equal to
D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}
by finality of the
coalgebra
(DA,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})
.

Finally, let us show commutativity of the top cell. Using the fact that
\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

is an isomorphism, this amounts to showing

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\hskip 3.44444pt\langle D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},D\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}\rangle\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}}

\displaystyle\,=\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\times\mathsf{id}),

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\hskip 3.44444pt\langle D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},D\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}\rangle\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}

\displaystyle\,=\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\lat\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}),D\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}\rangle.

Indeed,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}

\displaystyle\times\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\hskip 3.44444pt\langle D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},D\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}\rangle\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}}

\displaystyle\;=\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\hskip 3.44444pt\langle D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},D\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}\rangle\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}

\displaystyle\;=\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}\rangle

\displaystyle\;=\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}}\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}\rangle

\displaystyle\;=\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}}\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}\rangle

\displaystyle\;=\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}),

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}

\displaystyle\times\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\hskip 3.44444pt\langle D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},D\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}\rangle\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}

\displaystyle\;=\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\hskip 3.44444pt\langle D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},D\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}\rangle\hskip 3.44444pt\lat

\displaystyle\;=\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\lat\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}),\lat\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}})\rangle

\displaystyle\;=\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}}\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\lat\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}),\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}})\rangle

\displaystyle\;=\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\lat\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}),\hskip 3.44444ptD\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}\rangle,

and we are done.
∎

A.16
Proof of
\cref
lem:elgot-iota

Indeed, consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(A\times{\rm Nature})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DDA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times{\rm Nature}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Da}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

where the square commutes, since
(DA,a)
is a
{\bm{\mathsf{D}}}
-algebra (
\cref
prop:uniform-iteration-eq) and the triangle commutes, because
a\hskip 3.44444pt\iota=\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}
, which easily follows
by induction.
∎

A.17
Proof of
\cref
lem:bis

First, note that

\displaystyle\rho\hskip 3.44444pt{[\eta,\,[\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},\,\iota\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{s}})]]}^{\star}

\displaystyle\;=\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\iota^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt{[\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{!}\rangle,\,[\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{!}\rangle\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},\,\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}]]}^{\star}

\displaystyle\;=\rho\hskip 3.44444ptD\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}\hskip 3.44444pt{[\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{!}\rangle,\,[\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{!}\rangle\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},\,\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}]]}^{\star}

\displaystyle\;=\rho\hskip 3.44444pt{[\eta,\,[\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},\,\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}]]}^{\star}.

By combining it with a symmetric argument, we obtain that

\rho\hskip 3.44444pt{[\eta,\,[\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},\,\iota\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{s}})]]}^{\star}=\rho\hskip 3.44444pt{[\eta,\,[\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},\,\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}]]}^{\star}=\rho\hskip 3.44444pt{[\eta,\,[\iota\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}),\,\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}]]}^{\star}
.

Now, given a morphism
f\colon DX\to A
that equalizes
\rho\hskip 3.44444pt{[\eta,\,[\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},\,\iota\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{s}})]]}^{\star}

and
\rho\hskip 3.44444pt{[\eta,\,[\iota\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}),\,\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}]]}^{\star}
, we obtain that

f\hskip 3.44444pt\iota^{\star}=f\hskip 3.44444pt{[\eta,\,[\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},\,\iota\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{s}})]]}^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptD\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}=f\hskip 3.44444pt{[\eta,\,[\iota\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}),\,\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}]]}^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptD\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}=f\hskip 3.44444ptD\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}
,
by assumption, there is a unique factorization of
f
through
\rho
.
∎

A.18
Proof of
\cref
thm:Drho

We show the following implications:

1
.
\Rightarrow
2
.
\Rightarrow
3
.
\Rightarrow
1
.

and

3
.
\Rightarrow
4
.
\Rightarrow
2
.

1
.
\Rightarrow
2
.

Using the assumption, we define
\alpha_{X}\colon D{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X\to{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X
by the
universal property:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DD(X\times{\rm Nature})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DDX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DD\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\iota^{\star}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\rho_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{X}}

where we call on the calculation:

\displaystyle\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\mu\hskip 3.44444ptD\iota^{\star}=\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\mu\hskip 3.44444ptD\mu\hskip 3.44444ptDD\iota=\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\mu\hskip 3.44444pt\mu\hskip 3.44444ptDD\iota=\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\mu\hskip 3.44444ptD\iota\hskip 3.44444pt\mu=\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\iota^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\mu=\rho\hskip 3.44444ptD\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}\hskip 3.44444pt\mu=\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\mu\hskip 3.44444ptDD\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}.

By definition,
({\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X,\alpha_{X})
is a
D
-algebra and
\rho_{X}
is a
D
-algebra
morphism. Let us show that
({\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X,\alpha_{X})
is a search-algebra, i.e. that

\alpha_{X}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}=\mathsf{id}
and
\alpha_{X}\hskip 3.44444pt\lat=\alpha_{X}
. For the first equation,
note that
\alpha_{X}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\hskip 3.44444pt\rho=\alpha_{X}\hskip 3.44444ptD\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}=\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\mu\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}=\rho
,
which entails
\alpha_{X}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}=\mathsf{id}
using the fact that
\rho
is an epi.
Analogously,
\alpha_{X}\hskip 3.44444pt\lat\hskip 3.44444ptD\rho=\alpha_{X}\hskip 3.44444ptD\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\lat=\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\mu\hskip 3.44444pt\lat=\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\lat\hskip 3.44444pt\mu=\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\mu=\alpha_{X}\hskip 3.44444ptD\rho
using Lemmas
39

and
33
, and again, we are done by discarding
D\rho
, which
is epic by assumption.

2
.
\Rightarrow
3
.

Let
({\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X,\alpha_{X}\colon D{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X\to{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X)
be a search-algebra structure,
which exists by assumption. To show that
{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X
is an Elgot algebra, by
\cref
prop:uniform-iteration-eq, it suffices to show that
it is a
{\bm{\mathsf{D}}}
-algebra, i.e.
\alpha_{X}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}=\mathsf{id}
, which is by assumption,
and
\alpha_{X}\hskip 3.44444pt\mu=\alpha_{X}\hskip 3.44444ptD\alpha_{X}
, which we can prove, using the
assumption that
\rho
is a
D
-algebra morphism and assuming for the
time being that
DD\rho
is epic, as follows:

\alpha_{X}\hskip 3.44444pt\mu\hskip 3.44444ptDD\rho=\alpha_{X}\hskip 3.44444ptD\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\mu=\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\mu\hskip 3.44444pt\mu=\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\mu\hskip 3.44444ptD\mu=\alpha_{X}\hskip 3.44444ptD\rho\hskip 3.44444ptD\mu=\alpha_{X}\hskip 3.44444ptD\alpha_{X}\hskip 3.44444ptDD\rho
. The proof that
DD\rho
is epic is entailed
by the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DX\times D\bar{\mathbb{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X\times D\bar{\mathbb{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(DX\times\bar{\mathbb{N}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D({\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X\times\bar{\mathbb{N}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DD(DX\times 1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DD({\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X\times 1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho\times\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\rho\times\mathsf{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DD(\rho\times\mathsf{id})}

where, up to the obvious isomorphisms, our morphism of interest is the horizontal bottom one.
To show that it is epic, it suffices to show that any path from the left top corner to the right bottom corner is epic, specifically, we consider the composition
D\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\rho\times\mathsf{id})
.
This is epic, because
\rho\times\mathsf{id}
is a coequalizer and the involved
\tau
are
retractions by
\cref
lem:tau-retr.

To prove that
\rho=((\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\sharp}
, note that,
by definition,
((\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\sharp}=\alpha_{X}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}((\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})
.
It is easy to see by the universal property of
\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}
that

\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}((\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})=D(\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}})
. Hence
((\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\sharp}=\alpha_{X}\hskip 3.44444ptD(\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}})
, which is equal to
\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\mu\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{\mathsf{now}})=\rho
,
since, by assumption,
\rho
is a
D
-algebra morphism.

Finally, let us show freeness. Given an Elgot algebra
A
and
f\colon X\times Y\to A
, we provide a
unique right iteration preserving
f^{\hash}\colon X\times{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}Y\to A
such that
f=f^{\hash}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}})
.
Using
\cref
prop:uniform-iteration-eq, again, we assume a search-algebra
(A,a)
such that
{a\colon DA\to A}
is a
{\bm{\mathsf{D}}}
-algebra. We define
f^{\hash}
by a universal property
from the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times D(Y\times{\rm Nature})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times DY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DA}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\times D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\times\iota^{\star}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\times\rho_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Df)\hskip 2.41112pt\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\hash}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

which is justified by the following calculation:

\displaystyle a\hskip 3.44444pt(Df)\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\iota^{\star})\,

\displaystyle=a\hskip 3.44444pt\iota^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptD(f\times\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444ptD\operatorname{\mathsf{assoc}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau

\displaystyle=a\hskip 3.44444ptD\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}\hskip 3.44444ptD(f\times\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444ptD\operatorname{\mathsf{assoc}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau

/​​/
\cref
lem:elgot-iota

\displaystyle=a\hskip 3.44444ptDf\hskip 3.44444ptD\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}\hskip 3.44444ptD\operatorname{\mathsf{assoc}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau

\displaystyle=a\hskip 3.44444ptDf\hskip 3.44444ptD(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau

\displaystyle=a\hskip 3.44444pt(Df)\hskip 3.44444ptD(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau

\displaystyle=a\hskip 3.44444pt(Df)\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}})

We then immediately have

\displaystyle f^{\hash}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}})\,

\displaystyle=a\hskip 3.44444pt(Df)\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{now}})

\displaystyle=a\hskip 3.44444pt(Df)\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}

\displaystyle=a\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\hskip 3.44444ptf

\displaystyle=f.

Let us show that
f^{\hash}
is right iteration preserving, i.e. given

g\colon Z\to{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}Y+Z
,

\displaystyle f^{\hash}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\alpha_{Y}\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}g))=a\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}((f^{\hash}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times g)).

First, we show that

\displaystyle f^{\hash}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\alpha_{Y})=a\hskip 3.44444pt(Df^{\hash})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau.

To that end we compose both sides with
\mathsf{id}\times D\rho
, and make use the fact that
it is an epi.

\displaystyle f^{\hash}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\alpha_{Y})\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times D\rho)\,

\displaystyle=f^{\hash}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\mu)

\displaystyle=a\hskip 3.44444pt(Df)\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\mu)

\displaystyle=a\hskip 3.44444pt(Df)\hskip 3.44444pt\mu\hskip 3.44444pt(D\tau)\hskip 3.44444pt\tau

\displaystyle=a\hskip 3.44444pt\mu\hskip 3.44444pt(DDf)\hskip 3.44444pt(D\tau)\hskip 3.44444pt\tau

\displaystyle=a\hskip 3.44444pt(Da)\hskip 3.44444pt(DDf)\hskip 3.44444pt(D\tau)\hskip 3.44444pt\tau

\displaystyle=a\hskip 3.44444pt(Df^{\hash})\hskip 3.44444ptD(\mathsf{id}\times\rho)\hskip 3.44444pt\tau

\displaystyle=a\hskip 3.44444pt(Df^{\hash})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times D\rho).

This reduces the goal to

\displaystyle(Df^{\hash})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}g)=\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}((f^{\hash}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times g)),

and the latter follows from the fact that the left hand side satisfies the characteristic
equation for the right hand side:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\hskip 3.44444pt(D

\displaystyle f^{\hash})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}g)

\displaystyle=(f^{\hash}+Df^{\hash})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}g)

\displaystyle=(f^{\hash}+Df^{\hash})\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}+\tau)\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}g)

\displaystyle=(f^{\hash}+Df^{\hash})\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}+\tau)\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}g)\hskip 3.44444ptg)

\displaystyle=(f^{\hash}+Df^{\hash})\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}+\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}g))\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times g)

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{id}+(Df^{\hash})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}g))\hskip 3.44444pt(f^{\hash}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times g).

Finally, let
g\colon X\times{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}Y\to A
be right iteration preserving, such
that
f=g\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}})
and show that
g=f^{\hash}
. By definition of

f^{\hash}
, we need to show that
g\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\rho)=a\hskip 3.44444pt(Df)\hskip 3.44444pt\tau
. Using
the equation
\rho=((\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\sharp}
, we proved above,
we derive the goal as follows:

\displaystyle g\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\rho)\,

\displaystyle=g\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times((\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\sharp})

\displaystyle=((g+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times(\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}))^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=((g\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}})+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}))^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=((f+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}))^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=a\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}((f+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}))

\displaystyle=a\hskip 3.44444pt(Df)\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}))

\displaystyle=a\hskip 3.44444pt(Df)\hskip 3.44444pt\tau.

3
.
\Rightarrow
1
.

Let
({\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X,\operatorname{}\linebreak\alpha_{X}\colon D{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X\to{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X)
be the relevant Elgot
algebra structure, which exists by definition.
Let
w\colon DX\times\bar{\mathbb{N}}\to DX+DX\times\bar{\mathbb{N}}
be as follows:

\displaystyle w(p,\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\star)=\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}}p

\displaystyle w(p,\lat n)=\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}(\ear p,n).

Analogously, let
u\colon D(X\times{\rm Nature})\times\bar{\mathbb{N}}\to D(X\times{\rm Nature})+D(X\times{\rm Nature})\times\bar{\mathbb{N}}

be as follows:

\displaystyle u(p,\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\star)=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}}p,

\displaystyle u(\lat p,\lat n)=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}(p,n),

\displaystyle u(\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}(x,\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}),\lat n)=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}(x,\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}),n),

\displaystyle u(\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}(x,\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}(k)),\lat n)=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}(x,k),n).

We thus obtain two morphisms:
\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}w\colon DX\times\bar{\mathbb{N}}\to DDX
and

\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}u\colon D(X\times{\rm Nature})\times\bar{\mathbb{N}}\to DD(X\times{\rm Nature})
. To build intuition,
let us replace
X
with
1
. Then
\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}w\colon\bar{\mathbb{N}}\times\bar{\mathbb{N}}\to D\bar{\mathbb{N}}
essentially
computes truncated difference: it subtracts a possibly infinite second number

m
from a possibly infinite first number
n
and produces a process
D\bar{\mathbb{N}}
,
which runs
m
time units, and in case of termination returns the truncated
difference
n\dotdiv m
. Since subtraction is inverse to summation, this explains
why
\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}w
is a section, whose retraction is
\langle\mu,D\operatorname{!}\rangle\colon D\bar{\mathbb{N}}\to\bar{\mathbb{N}}\times\bar{\mathbb{N}}
,
which remains true for arbitrary
X
. The morphism
\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}u\colon D{\rm Nature}\times\bar{\mathbb{N}}\to DD{\rm Nature}

refines
\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}w
in the following sense. The first argument can be regarded
as a sum of a possibly infinite
n
with a finite
k
(so that
n+k=n
if
n
is
infinite) and then
\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}u
again computes the truncated difference
n+k\dotdiv m

in the form of a formal sum
(n\dotdiv m)+k
if
n
is greater than
m
and
(n+k)\dotdiv m

otherwise.

Now, consider the following diagram, which summarizes the argument.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DDX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(X\times{\rm Nature})\times\bar{\mathbb{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DX\times\bar{\mathbb{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X\times\bar{\mathbb{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DD(X\times{\rm Nature})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DDX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\rho_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mu,D\operatorname{!}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\alpha,\operatorname{}\linebreak D\operatorname{!}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}\times\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{\star}\times\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{X}\times\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}})\hskip 2.41112pt\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DD\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\iota^{\star}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\rho_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

We would like to show that
D\rho
is a coequalizer of the bottom parallel
pair of morphisms. To that end, we fix
a\colon D{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X\to Y
, such that
a\hskip 3.44444pt\iota^{\star}=a\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}})

and construct such
{b\colon D{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X\to Y}
that
a=b\hskip 3.44444pt(D\rho)
. Assuming,
for the moment, that
all rectangular cells (with coherently chosen edges of the corresponding parallel
pairs) commute, we obtain that
a\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}w)
coequalizes
\iota^{\star},D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}
,
which produces a suitable
c
, by a coequalizer property. Let
b=c\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\alpha,D\operatorname{!}\rangle

and using the fact that the two vertical morphisms from
DDX
to
DDX
and from

D{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X
to
D{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X
are identities, obtain the desired equation
b\hskip 3.44444ptD\rho=a
.
The fact that the vertical morphism from
D{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X
to
D{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X
is the identity
is by
\cref
lem:tau-retr.
The fact that the vertical morphism from
DDX
to
DDX
is the identity, we
show directly. We will show that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}w)\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mu,D\operatorname{!}\rangle=(\mathsf{id}+(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}w)\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mu,D\operatorname{!}\rangle)\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

This identifies
(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}w)\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mu,D\operatorname{!}\rangle
as a unique final coalgebra morphism,
which thus must be equal to
\mathsf{id}
. Since

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}w)\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mu,D\operatorname{!}\rangle=(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}w)\hskip 3.44444ptw\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mu,D\operatorname{!}\rangle,

we reduce the previous equation to

\displaystyle w\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mu,D\operatorname{!}\rangle=(\mathsf{id}+\langle\mu,D\operatorname{!}\rangle)\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

By composing both sides with
\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}
and
\lat
correspondingly,
we reduce to

\displaystyle w\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{!}\rangle=\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}},

\displaystyle w\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mu\hskip 3.44444pt\lat,(D\operatorname{!})\hskip 3.44444pt\lat\rangle=\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mu,D\operatorname{!}\rangle.

The first equation directly follows by definition of
w
. For the second equation,

w\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mu\hskip 3.44444pt\lat,(D\operatorname{!})\hskip 3.44444pt\lat\rangle=w\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\lat\hskip 3.44444pt\mu,\lat\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{!})\rangle=\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mu,D\operatorname{!}\rangle
, using
\cref
lem:later (1).

We proceed to show non-trivial commutativity conditions for the square cells
of our diagram. These are the following:

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}w)\hskip 3.44444pt(\iota^{\star}\times\mathsf{id})=

\displaystyle\;D\iota^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}u)

(12)

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}w)\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}\times\mathsf{id})=

\displaystyle\;(DD\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}})\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}u)

(13)

\displaystyle(D\rho)\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}w)=

\displaystyle\;(D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\rho\times\mathsf{id})

(14)

To obtain (
12
), we show that both sides are equal to

\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}((\iota^{\star}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444ptu)
, which in turn amounts to proving that the left and
the right hand are both universal coalgebra maps, i.e.

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\hskip 3.44444pt(D\iota^{\star})\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}u)=

\displaystyle\;(\mathsf{id}+(D\iota^{\star})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}u)\hskip 3.44444pt(\iota^{\star}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444ptu

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}w)\hskip 3.44444pt(\iota^{\star}\times\mathsf{id})=

\displaystyle\;(\mathsf{id}+(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}w)\hskip 3.44444pt(\iota^{\star}\times\mathsf{id}))\hskip 3.44444pt(\iota^{\star}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444ptu

The first one is obvious, and we proceed with the second one. Since

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}w)\hskip 3.44444pt(\iota^{\star}\times\mathsf{id})=

\displaystyle\;(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}w)\hskip 3.44444ptw\hskip 3.44444pt(\iota^{\star}\times\mathsf{id}),

we are left to show that

\displaystyle(\iota^{\star}+\iota^{\star}\times\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444ptu=w\hskip 3.44444pt(\iota^{\star}\times\mathsf{id}).

By case distinction:

\displaystyle(\iota^{\star}+\iota^{\star}\times\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt(u(p,\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\star))=

\displaystyle\;(\iota^{\star}+\iota^{\star}\times\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}}p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}}(\iota^{\star}(p))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;w(\iota^{\star}(p),\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}\star),

\displaystyle(\iota^{\star}+\iota^{\star}\times\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt(u(\lat p,\lat n))=

\displaystyle\;(\iota^{\star}+\iota^{\star}\times\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}(p,n))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}(\iota^{\star}(p),n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}(\ear(\iota^{\star}(\lat p)),n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;w\hskip 3.44444pt(\iota^{\star}(\lat p),\lat n),

\displaystyle(\iota^{\star}+\iota^{\star}\times\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt(u(\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}(x,\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}),\lat n))=

\displaystyle\;(\iota^{\star}+\iota^{\star}\times\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}(x,\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}),n))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}(\iota^{\star}(\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}(x,\operatorname{\mathsf{o}})),n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}(\iota(x,\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}),n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}x,n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}(\ear(\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}x),n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}(\ear(\iota^{\star}(\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}(x,\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}))),n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;w\hskip 3.44444pt(\iota^{\star}(\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}(x,\operatorname{\mathsf{o}})),\lat n),

\displaystyle(\iota^{\star}+\iota^{\star}\times\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt(u(\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}(x,\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}(k)),\lat n))=

\displaystyle\;(\iota^{\star}+\iota^{\star}\times\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}(x,k),n))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}(\iota^{\star}(\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}(x,k)),n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}(\iota(x,k),n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}(\ear(\iota(x,\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}(k))),n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}(\ear(\iota^{\star}(\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}(x,\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}(k)))),n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;w\hskip 3.44444pt(\iota^{\star}(\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}(x,\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}(k))),\lat n).

The proof of (
13
) runs analogously. To obtain (
14
), again,
we show that both sides are equal to
\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}((\rho+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444ptw)
, by establishing equations

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\hskip 3.44444pt(D\rho)\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}w)=

\displaystyle\;(\mathsf{id}+(D\rho)\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}w)\hskip 3.44444pt(\rho+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444ptw,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\rho\times\mathsf{id})=

\displaystyle\;(\mathsf{id}+(D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\rho\times\mathsf{id}))\hskip 3.44444pt(\rho+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444ptw.

The first equation is again easy to see. Since

\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\rho\times\mathsf{id})=(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+(D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau)\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\rho\times\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})=(\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+(D\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\rho\times\mathsf{id}))\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})
,
we reduce to

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\rho\times\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})=(\mathsf{id}+\rho\times\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444ptw.

This is however easy to see by definition of
w
.

Finally, we have to show that
b
is the unique morphism for which
a=b\hskip 3.44444pt(D\rho)
.
We obtain this by proving that
D\rho
is an epi. To this end, consider the
following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces DX\times\bar{\mathbb{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X\times\bar{\mathbb{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(DX\times 1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D({\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X\times 1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho\times\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\rho\times\mathsf{id})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

where we assume that
f\hskip 3.44444ptD(\rho\times\mathsf{id})=g\hskip 3.44444ptD(\rho\times\mathsf{id})
.
Then, also
f\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\rho\times\mathsf{id})=g\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\rho\times\mathsf{id})
.
Since
\rho\times\mathsf{id}
is an epi, and
\tau
is a retraction by

\cref
lem:tau-retr, their composition is epic, hence
f=g
. We have thus shown
that
D(\rho\times\mathsf{id})
is epic, and since
DY\times 1\mathbin{\cong}DY
, so
is
D\rho
.

3
.
\Rightarrow
4
.

The proof that
{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}
extends to a monad is analogous to that of

\cref
lem:b-mon. Strength is defined and characterized in the same way
as in
\cref
prop:tau-prop. We are left to show that
\rho
is a strong
monad morphism. By definition,
\rho
respects monad unit. Let us show that it
respects multiplication, i.e.
\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\mu=\mu\hskip 3.44444pt\rho\hskip 3.44444ptD\rho
. By assumption,
every
\rho_{X}
is a
D
-algebra morphism, i.e.
\rho_{X}\hskip 3.44444pt\mu_{X}=\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\sharp}\hskip 3.44444ptD\rho_{X}

(using the definition of the
D
-algebra structure for
{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X
from

\cref
prop:uniform-iteration-eq). Thus, we are left to show that

\mu\hskip 3.44444pt\rho=\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\sharp}
. By assumption,
\rho=((\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\sharp}
, and
\mu\colon{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X\to{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X
is
iteration preserving, hence

\displaystyle\mu\hskip 3.44444pt\rho=\mu\hskip 3.44444pt((\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\sharp}=((\mu\hskip 3.44444pt\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\sharp}=\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\sharp}.

Finally, let us show that
\rho
preserves strength, i.e.
\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\tau=\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\rho)
.
Using assumption
\rho=((\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\sharp}
, we have

\displaystyle\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\rho)=

\displaystyle\;\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times((\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\sharp})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;((\tau+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times(\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}))^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;((\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}})+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}))^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;((\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dstr}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}))^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;((\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{now}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\sharp}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\tau.

where the second to last step is the characterization of
\tau
from

\cref
prop:D-props and
Uniformity
.

4
.
\Rightarrow
2
.

Suppose that
{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}
extends to a strong monad
{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{{\bm{\mathsf{D}}}}}
and
\rho
to a strong monad
morphism. Let us define the search-algebra structure on
{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X
as

\alpha_{X}=\mu_{X}\hskip 3.44444pt\rho_{{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X}\colon D{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X\to{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}X
. The axioms of search-algebras follow
by definition and by
\cref
lem:rho-eq-lat. By assumption,
\rho
is a monad
morphism, in particular,
\rho\hskip 3.44444pt\mu=\mu\hskip 3.44444pt\rho\hskip 3.44444ptD\rho
, hence

\rho_{X}\hskip 3.44444pt\mu_{X}=\mu_{X}\hskip 3.44444pt\rho_{DX}\hskip 3.44444ptD\rho_{X}=\alpha_{X}\hskip 3.44444ptD\rho_{X}
, i.e.
\rho_{X}

is a
D
-algebra morphism.
∎

A.19
Proof of
\cref
thm:elg

The difficult clause is
2
.
In order to show it,
some preparatory work is needed.

Lemma 49
.

Suppose that the equivalent conditions of
\cref
thm:Drho hold. Then
\Sigma={\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}1

is an internal distributive lattice with
\bot\colon 1\to{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}1
as the bottom,

\top=\eta\colon 1\to{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}1
as the top and
\land={\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}\operatorname{!}\hskip 3.44444pt\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\colon{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}1\times{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}1\to{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}1
.

Proof.

T1
is in fact a meet-semilattice for any equational lifting monad
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
with
a point
\bot\colon 1\to T\iobj
, which is easy to check. We proceed to define binary
joins
\lor\colon\Sigma\times\Sigma\to\Sigma
in two steps. First, we define an
auxiliary map
j\colon\bar{\mathbb{N}}\times\Sigma\to\bar{\mathbb{N}}
as a universal arrow from the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\mathbb{N}}\times D{\rm Nature}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\mathbb{N}}\times\bar{\mathbb{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\mathbb{N}}\times\Sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D{\rm Nature}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\mathbb{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\times D\operatorname{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\times\hat{\iota}^{\star}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D[\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\operatorname{!},t]}\hskip 2.41112pt\zeta_{1,{\rm Nature}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\times\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{D\operatorname{!}}\hskip 2.41112pt\zeta_{1,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\operatorname{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\iota}^{\star}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

where
\zeta_{X,Y}\colon DX\times DY\to D(X\times DY+DX\times Y)
runs the arguments
in parallel until one of the computations terminates and
t\colon\bar{\mathbb{N}}\times{\rm Nature}\to{\rm Nature}

returns the minimum of the first and the second arguments, concretely, by primitive
recursion:

\displaystyle t(p,\operatorname{\mathsf{o}})=\operatorname{\mathsf{o}},\qquad t(p,\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}(n))=\operatorname{\mathsf{case}}\mathbin{}(\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}p)\operatorname{\mathsf{of}}\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}}\star\mapsto\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\operatorname{\mathsf{\kern-1.0pt;}}\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}}q\mapsto\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}(t(q,n)).

Then we define
\lor
from the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D{\rm Nature}\times\bar{\mathbb{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\mathbb{N}}\times\bar{\mathbb{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma\times\bar{\mathbb{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D{\rm Nature}\times\Sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\mathbb{N}}\times\Sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma\times\Sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\operatorname{!}\times\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\iota}^{\star}\times\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\times\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho\times\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\times\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\times\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\operatorname{!}\times\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\iota}^{\star}\times\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho\times\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lor}

where the fact that
j
equalizes
D\operatorname{!}\times\mathsf{id}
and
\hat{\iota}^{\star}\times\mathsf{id}

follows from the next calculation and the fact that
\rho\times\mathsf{id}
is epic:

\displaystyle j\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{!}\times\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\rho)

\displaystyle\;=j\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\rho)\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{!}\times\mathsf{id})

\displaystyle\;=j\hskip 3.44444pt\rho\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{!})\hskip 3.44444pt\zeta_{1,1}\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{!}\times\mathsf{id})

\displaystyle\;=j\hskip 3.44444pt\rho\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{!})\hskip 3.44444pt\zeta_{1,1}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{swap}}\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{!}\times\mathsf{id})

\displaystyle\;=j\hskip 3.44444pt\rho\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{!})\hskip 3.44444pt\zeta_{1,1}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times D\operatorname{!})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{swap}}

\displaystyle\;=j\hskip 3.44444pt\rho\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{!})\hskip 3.44444pt\zeta_{1,1}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\hat{\iota}^{\star})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{swap}}

\displaystyle\;=j\hskip 3.44444pt\rho\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{!})\hskip 3.44444pt\zeta_{1,1}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{swap}}\hskip 3.44444pt(\hat{\iota}^{\star}\times\mathsf{id})

\displaystyle\;=j\hskip 3.44444pt\rho\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{!})\hskip 3.44444pt\zeta_{1,1}\hskip 3.44444pt(\hat{\iota}^{\star}\times\mathsf{id})

\displaystyle\;=j\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\rho)\hskip 3.44444pt(\hat{\iota}^{\star}\times\mathsf{id})

\displaystyle\;=j\hskip 3.44444pt(\hat{\iota}^{\star}\times\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times\rho)

where we used the obvious fact that
\zeta
is commutative. By definition,

\displaystyle\lor\hskip 3.44444pt(\rho\times\rho)=\rho\hskip 3.44444pt(D\operatorname{!})\hskip 3.44444pt\zeta_{1,1}

which immediately entails that
\lor
is commutative using the fact that
\rho\times\rho

is epic. In a similar way, we can transfer the distributivity law

a\land(b\lor c)=(a\land b)\lor(a\land c)
. To that end, we use the following
analogue of that law for
{\bm{\mathsf{D}}}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\mathbb{N}}\times(\bar{\mathbb{N}}\times\bar{\mathbb{N}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bar{\mathbb{N}}\times\bar{\mathbb{N}})\times(\bar{\mathbb{N}}\times\bar{\mathbb{N}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\mathbb{N}}\times\bar{\mathbb{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\mathbb{N}}\times\bar{\mathbb{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\mathbb{N}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}\times(D\operatorname{!})\hskip 2.41112pt\zeta_{1,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},~\mathsf{id}\times\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((D\operatorname{!})\hskip 2.41112pt\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 2.41112pt\tau)\times((D\operatorname{!})\hskip 2.41112pt\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 2.41112pt\tau)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(D\operatorname{!})\hskip 2.41112pt\zeta_{1,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(D\operatorname{!})\hskip 2.41112pt\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 2.41112pt\tau}

This law essentially states that the operation of minimum on
\bar{\mathbb{N}}
distributes
over the operation of summation. By postcomposing this law with
\rho
and using
the property that
\rho\times(\rho\times\rho)
is epi again, we obtain the
desired distributivity law for
\Sigma
. In a similar way we obtain the absorption
law
a\lor(a\land b)=a
.

The laws that we obtained are sufficient to show that
\lor
is a join. On the one hand,
by the absorption law
a\land(a\lor b)=(a\land a)\lor(a\land b)=a\lor(a\land b)=a
,
which is equivalent to
a\leq a\lor b
, and analogously,
b\leq a\lor b
. On the
other hand, if
a\leq c
and
b\leq c
, then
a\land c=a
,
b\land c=b
and hence

(a\lor b)\land c=(a\land b)\lor(a\land c)=a\lor b
, i.e.
a\lor b\leq c
.
∎

Lemma 50
.

Suppose that the equivalent conditions of
\cref
thm:Drho hold, and additionally
the coequalizer (
2
) with
X=1
is preserved by

(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\rm Nature}
. Then
\Sigma
is an internal
\omega
-frame, i.e. there is
an
\omega
-join
\bigvee\colon\Sigma^{\rm Nature}\to\Sigma
, which satisfying the frame
distributive law
a\land\bigvee_{i}b_{i}=\bigvee_{i}(a\land b_{i})
.

Proof.

The approach is similar to that of Lemma
49
: we construct

\bigvee\colon\Sigma^{\rm Nature}\to\Sigma
by the following universal property

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(D{\rm Nature})^{\rm Nature}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\mathbb{N}}^{\rm Nature}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{\rm Nature}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{\mathbb{N}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(D\operatorname{!})^{\rm Nature}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\hat{\iota}^{\star})^{\rm Nature}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{\rm Nature}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigvee}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

where the morphism
\bar{\mathbb{N}}^{\rm Nature}\to\bar{\mathbb{N}}
converts a countable collections of
infinite streams into a single stream, which is possible using the standard
diagonalization argument.
∎

Recall the bounded iteration operator
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\langle\kern-0.8pt\sharp}
from
\cref
def:biter.
For a monad
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
with a constant
\bot\colon 1\to TX
, we can instantiate this definition
in the Kleisli category
\mathbf{C}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}}
of
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
, which yields
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\langle\kern-0.8pt\dagger}\colon\mathbf{C}(X,T(Y+X))\to\mathbf{C}(X\times{\rm Nature},TY)
for any
Y
(because every
Y
has a point

\bot\colon 1\to TY
in
\mathbf{C}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}}
).

Lemma 51
.

Let
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
be an equational lifting monad, and suppose that it is equipped with
an operator
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\dagger}\colon\mathbf{C}(X,T(Y+X))\to\mathbf{C}(X,TY)
that satisfies

Fixpoint
,
Naturality
,
Uniformity
and
Strength
. This induces a divergence constant
\bot=(\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}})^{\dagger}\colon 1\to TX
.

Given
f\colon X\to T(Y+X)
, and
g\colon X\to TY
, (i)
f^{{\langle\kern-0.8pt\dagger}}\sqsubseteq f^{\dagger}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}
,
and (ii)
f^{{\langle\kern-0.8pt\dagger}}\sqsubseteq g\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}
implies
f^{\dagger}\sqsubseteq g
.

Proof.

The clauses (i) and (ii) are analogous to those of
\cref
thm:klee.
The first clause is shown in exactly the same way. For the second clause, analogously,
we define
h\colon X\times{\rm Nature}\to T({\rm Nature}+X\times{\rm Nature})
with the property that

\displaystyle f^{\dagger}=(f^{\langle\kern-0.8pt\dagger})^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},h^{\dagger}\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{!}\rangle\rangle

(15)

which will entail (ii) using the same argument as in
\cref
thm:klee, using
Naturality
and
Strength
. Here, we take

\displaystyle h=T(\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt(T\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}})\hskip 3.44444pt\hat{\tau}\hskip 3.44444pt(f\times\operatorname{\mathsf{s}})

This function runs just like
f
, but produces the number of computation steps
as a final result. For the sake of brevity, let us denote
h^{\dagger}\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{!}\rangle\colon X\to T{\rm Nature}

by
c
. First of all, we show the following auxiliary property:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}f^{\dagger}=\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}c.

(16)

It easily follows by
Naturality
and
Uniformity
that
(T\operatorname{!})\hskip 3.44444pth^{\dagger}=(T(\operatorname{!}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444ptf)^{\dagger}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}
.
Then

\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}(h^{\dagger}\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{!}\rangle)=\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}((T\operatorname{!})\hskip 3.44444pth^{\dagger}\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{!}\rangle)=\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}((T(\operatorname{!}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444ptf)^{\dagger}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{!}\rangle)=\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}f^{\dagger}
.

The main step of the proof is showing commutativity of the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(Y+X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times T{\rm Nature}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(Y+X\times T{\rm Nature})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\star}\hskip 2.41112pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}f^{\dagger})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau\hskip 2.41112pt\langle\mathsf{id},c\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(\mathsf{id}+\tau\hskip 2.41112pt\langle\mathsf{id},c\rangle)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\eta)\hskip 2.41112pt(T\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}})\hskip 2.41112pt\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 2.41112pt(\tau\hskip 2.41112pt(f\times d))^{\star}}

where
d\colon{\rm Nature}\to T{\rm Nature}
is as follows:

\displaystyle d(\operatorname{\mathsf{o}})=\bot,

\displaystyle d(\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}n)=\eta(n).

By
Uniformity
, this entails the equation

\displaystyle(f^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}f^{\dagger}))^{\dagger}=(T(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\eta)\hskip 3.44444pt(T\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}})\hskip 3.44444pt\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt(\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(f\times d))^{\star})^{\dagger}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},c\rangle

(17)

Roughly, this says that iterating
f
is equivalent to iterating
f
and additionally checking
that the counter that is initialized by
c
and decreased at each iteration
remains non-zero. The term
\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}f^{\dagger}
is needed to balance the effect of recalculating

c
at each iteration out.
We have

\displaystyle T(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\eta)\hskip 3.44444pt

\displaystyle(T\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}})\hskip 3.44444pt\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt(\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(f\times d))^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},c\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;T(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\eta)\hskip 3.44444pt(T\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}})\hskip 3.44444pt\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(f\times d^{\star})\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},c\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;T(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\eta)\hskip 3.44444pt(T\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}})\hskip 3.44444pt\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle f,d^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptc\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;T(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\eta)\hskip 3.44444pt(T\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}})\hskip 3.44444pt\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle f,d^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt[\eta,h^{\dagger}]^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pth\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{!}\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;T(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\eta)\hskip 3.44444pt(T\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}})\hskip 3.44444pt\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle f,d^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt[\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\operatorname{!},h^{\dagger}\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\operatorname{!}\rangle]^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptf\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;T(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\eta)\hskip 3.44444pt(T\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}})\hskip 3.44444pt\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle f,[\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\operatorname{!},c]^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptf\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;T(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\eta)\hskip 3.44444pt(T\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}})\hskip 3.44444pt\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times[\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\operatorname{!},c]^{\star})\hskip 3.44444pt\langle f,f\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;T(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\eta)\hskip 3.44444pt(T\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\hat{\tau}\hskip 3.44444pt(\mathsf{id}\times[\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\operatorname{!},c]^{\star})\hskip 3.44444pt\langle f,f\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;T(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\eta)\hskip 3.44444pt(T\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptT(\mathsf{id}\times[\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\operatorname{!},c]^{\star})\hskip 3.44444pt\hat{\tau}\hskip 3.44444pt\langle f,f\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;T(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\eta)\hskip 3.44444pt(T\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptT(\mathsf{id}\times[\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\operatorname{!},c]^{\star})\hskip 3.44444ptT\langle\mathsf{id},\eta\rangle\hskip 3.44444ptf

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;T(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\eta)\hskip 3.44444pt(T\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptT\langle\mathsf{id},[\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\operatorname{!},c]\rangle\hskip 3.44444ptf

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;T(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\eta)\hskip 3.44444pt[(T\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{o}}\operatorname{!}\rangle,(T\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}})\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},c\rangle]^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptf

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}},(T\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}})\hskip 3.44444pt(T\eta)\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\langle\mathsf{id},c\rangle]^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptf

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}},(T\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}})\hskip 3.44444ptT(\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},c\rangle)\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}c]^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptf

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;T(\mathsf{id}+\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},c\rangle)\hskip 3.44444pt[\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}},(T\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}c]^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptf

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;T(\mathsf{id}+\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},c\rangle)\hskip 3.44444pt[\eta\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{inl}},(T\operatorname{\mathsf{inr}})\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}f^{\dagger}]^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptf

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;T(\mathsf{id}+\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},c\rangle)\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}[\eta,f^{\dagger}])^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptf

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;T(\mathsf{id}+\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},c\rangle)\hskip 3.44444ptf^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}([\eta,f^{\dagger}]^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptf)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;T(\mathsf{id}+\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},c\rangle)\hskip 3.44444ptf^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}f^{\dagger}).

The proof of (
15
) now runs as follows:

\displaystyle f^{\dagger}=

\displaystyle\;(f^{\dagger})^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}f^{\dagger})

/​​/
RST1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(f^{\dagger}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}f^{\dagger})^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}f^{\dagger})

/​​/
\cref
lem:dag-dom

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(f^{\dagger}\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}f^{\dagger})^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}c)

/​​/ (
16
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;((T(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\eta)\hskip 3.44444pt(T\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}})\hskip 3.44444pt\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt(\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(f\times d))^{\star})^{\dagger}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},c\rangle)^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt(\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}c)

/​​/ (
17
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;((T(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\eta)\hskip 3.44444pt(T\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}})\hskip 3.44444pt\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt(\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(f\times d))^{\star})^{\dagger})^{\star}

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374ptT(\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},c\rangle)\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{dom}}(\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},c\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;((T(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\eta)\hskip 3.44444pt(T\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}})\hskip 3.44444pt\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt(\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(f\times d))^{\star})^{\dagger})^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt(T\eta)\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},c\rangle

/​​/

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;((T(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\eta)\hskip 3.44444pt(T\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}})\hskip 3.44444pt\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt(\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(f\times d))^{\star})^{\dagger}\hskip 3.44444pt\eta)^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},c\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;((T(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt(T\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}})\hskip 3.44444pt\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(f\times d))^{\dagger})^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},c\rangle

Uniformity

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(f^{\langle\kern-0.8pt\dagger})^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt\langle\mathsf{id},c\rangle.

Here, the last step requires checking that
f^{\langle\kern-0.8pt\dagger}=(T(\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}+\mathsf{id})\hskip 3.44444pt(T\operatorname{\mathsf{dstl}})\hskip 3.44444pt\hat{\tau}^{\star}\hskip 3.44444pt\tau\hskip 3.44444pt(f\times d))^{\dagger}
,
which is easy, since

is defined by primitive recursion.
∎

Let us call a pair
(M\in|\mathbf{C}|,M_{\sqsubseteq}\subseteq M\times M)
a
(internal)
regular poset
if
M_{\sqsubseteq}
is a regular monic, i.e. an equalizer of some pair
l,r\colon M\times M\to E

and the relation
\sqsubseteq
on
\mathsf{Hom}(X,M)
, defined by putting
f\sqsubseteq g
iff

l\hskip 3.44444pt\langle f,g\rangle=r\hskip 3.44444pt\langle f,g\rangle
, is a partial order for every
X
. By definition,

f\sqsubseteq g
iff
\langle f,g\rangle
factors through
M_{\sqsubseteq}\subseteq M\times M
. Because
of the equalizer property, this factorization is unique. Our notion of a regular
poset is thus an instance of Barr’s notion of an (internal) poset
[
7
]
to
which we added the regularity requirement. We will follow Barr’s work further
on in internalizing further relevant notions of order theory. First, observe the
following.

Lemma 52
.

For every
X\in|\mathbf{C}|
, the equalizer of the pair
\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}},(\operatorname{\mathsf{snd}}^{\star})\hskip 3.44444pt\hat{\tau}\colon TX\times TX\to TX

defines a regular poset.

Proof.

Obvious by definition of the enrichment of
\mathbf{C}_{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
.
∎

Given a regular poset
(M\in|\mathbf{C}|,M_{\sqsubseteq}\subseteq M\times M)
, we define
the object of monotone sequences
M^{\omega}
from the pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\omega}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\rm Nature}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M_{\sqsubseteq})^{\rm Nature}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(M\times M)^{\rm Nature}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M^{\rm Nature}\times M^{\rm Nature}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathsf{id},M^{\operatorname{\mathsf{s}}}\rangle}
\cong

Now, a regular poset
(M\in|\mathbf{C}|,M_{\sqsubseteq}\subseteq M\times M)
is an internal
\omega
-cpo
if it is equipped with an operation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\bigsqcup$}}{\scalebox{.7}[1]{$\textstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\scalebox{.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\scalebox{.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}}\colon M^{\omega}\to M
, such that for every

f\colon X\to M^{\omega}
,
\mathop{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\bigsqcup$}}{\scalebox{.7}[1]{$\textstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\scalebox{.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\scalebox{.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}}\hskip 3.44444ptf\colon X\to M
is the least upper bound for

\hat{f}=\operatorname{\mathsf{curry}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}(X\,\xrightarrow{f}\,M^{\omega}\hookrightarrow M^{\rm Nature})
in
\mathbf{C}(X,M)
, meaning that
(i)
\hat{f}\sqsubseteq(\mathop{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\bigsqcup$}}{\scalebox{.7}[1]{$\textstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\scalebox{.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\scalebox{.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}}\hskip 3.44444ptf)\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}
and (ii)
\hat{f}\sqsubseteq g\hskip 3.44444pt\operatorname{\mathsf{fst}}
implies

\mathop{\mathchoice{\scalebox{.7}[1]{$\displaystyle\bigsqcup$}}{\scalebox{.7}[1]{$\textstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\scalebox{.7}[1]{$\scriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}{\scalebox{.7}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigsqcup$}}}\hskip 3.44444ptf\sqsubseteq g
for any
g\colon X\to M
.

Proposition 53
.

An equational lifting monad
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
is a strong Elgot monad if
T{\rm Nature}
is an internal

\omega
-cpo.

Proof.

First, given
f\colon X\to T({\rm Nature}+X)
, we define
f^{\dagger}\colon X\to T{\rm Nature}
as the
least fixpoint of the map
[\eta,\operatorname{-\!-}]^{\star}\hskip 3.44444ptf\colon\mathbf{C}(X,T{\rm Nature})\to\mathbf{C}(X,T{\rm Nature})
.
Then we extend
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\dagger}
to all
f\colon X\to T(Y+X)
using the
formula (
15
) from
\cref
lem:eql-elgot.
Fixpoint
,
Naturality
,
Uniformity
and
Strength
follow from this definition, which enables the characterization of
\cref
lem:eql-elgot,
and then
Compositionality
follows analogously to an existing argument
[
21
, Theorem 5.8]
.
∎

Finally, we proceed with the proof of
\cref
thm:elg.

1
.

In extensive categories coequalizers are preserved by
(\operatorname{-\!-}+1)
, hence, by
assumption, (
2
) is preserved by
(\operatorname{-\!-}+1)^{\rm Nature}
. Recall
that
DX
is a retract of
(X+1)^{\rm Nature}
naturally in
X
. It is easy to verify
that coequalizers are preserved by retractions, hence (
2
) is
preserved by
D
.

2
.

The claim follows from
\cref
prop:elg-form-join and
\cref
lem:eql-elgot. We
only need to extend
{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}{\rm Nature}
to an
\omega
-cpo. The effectiveness assumption
implies that
{\accentset{\scalebox{.9}[1]{\texttildelow}}{D}}{\rm Nature}
is isomorphic to
\Sigma^{\omega}
. By
\cref
lem:frame,
we have internal joins on
\Sigma
, which extend to
\Sigma^{\omega}
pointwise.
∎
